#  Batch 1: LED Patterns 1

## Project 0001: Introduction to LED Patterns

### 2. Learning Objective
Learn how to control digital outputs and implement timing loops to create repeating LED patterns.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Digital Output (High/Low)** (Used in Step 8)
*   **Timing delays** (Used in Step 8)
*   **Standard GPIO Control** (Used in Step 8)
*   **Infinite Loops (`while True`)** (Used in Step 8)

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **On-board LED**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **On-board LED** | GP25 (or "LED") | Standard Pico uses GP25; Pico W uses "LED" string |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Logic & Math, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin to ...)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_wait`** (wait)

### 7. Variables
*   **led**: Pin object assigned from `Pin(25, Pin.OUT)` representing the on-board LED

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**

1.  **Initialize LED Pin**:
    *   This project uses direct Python initialization: `led = Pin(25, Pin.OUT)`
    *   In Blockly, this translates to:
        *   From the **Variables** category in the left block palette, click **"Create variable..."**
        *   Name the variable **`led`**
        *   From **Variables**, drag the **`set [led] to`** block
        *   From **Smart IO** category, find the initialization blocks section
        *   **Note**: For this basic project, we use direct pin assignment in code
        *   The variable `led` will be created with value: `Pin(25, Pin.OUT)`
        *   Snap this into the **initialization area** (before any loops)

**B. Main Loop Phase**

2.  **Create Forever Loop**:
    *   From the **Logic & Math** category in the left block palette, locate and drag the **`pico_forever`** block (labeled "forever do")
    *   This block creates an infinite loop equivalent to Python's `while True:`
    *   Snap this block below the initialization section
    *   All subsequent steps will be placed **inside** this forever loop

3.  **Turn LED ON**:
    *   From the **Smart IO** category, locate and drag the **`pico_gpio_write`** block (labeled "set Pin [#] to [state]")
    *   Click on the block to reveal its parameter fields
    *   In the **Pin** number field (first field), enter **`25`**
        *   This corresponds to GP25 where the on-board LED is connected
        *   For Pico W, you may need to use the string `"LED"` instead
    *   In the **State** dropdown field (second field), select **HIGH (1)** from the dropdown options
        *   HIGH (1) means the pin outputs 3.3V, which turns the LED on
    *   **Snap** this block inside the `pico_forever` loop (first block in the loop)

4.  **Wait While LED is ON**:
    *   From the **Smart IO** category, locate and drag the **`pico_wait`** block (labeled "wait [#] [unit]")
    *   Click on the block to reveal its parameter fields
    *   In the **duration** number field (first field), enter **`2`**
    *   In the **unit** dropdown field (second field), select **`seconds`** from the options
        *   This creates a 2-second delay while the LED remains on
    *   **Snap** this block into the loop directly below the "Turn LED ON" block

5.  **Turn LED OFF**:
    *   From the **Smart IO** category, drag another **`pico_gpio_write`** block
    *   In the **Pin** number field, enter **`25`** (same pin as step 3)
    *   In the **State** dropdown field, select **LOW (0)** from the dropdown options
        *   LOW (0) means the pin outputs 0V, which turns the LED off
    *   **Snap** this block into the loop directly below the first "Wait" block

6.  **Wait While LED is OFF**:
    *   From the **Smart IO** category, drag another **`pico_wait`** block
    *   In the **duration** field, enter **`2`**
    *   In the **unit** dropdown, select **`seconds`**
        *   This creates a 2-second delay while the LED remains off
    *   **Snap** this block into the loop directly below the "Turn LED OFF" block
    *   The loop will now automatically repeat, returning to step 3

**Execution Order Summary:**
- Initialize `led` → Start forever loop → LED ON → Wait 2s → LED OFF → Wait 2s → (repeat)

### 9. Execution Flow

1.  **Startup (0-1 seconds)**:
    *   When the Pico powers on, the program initializes
    *   The on-board LED pin (GP25) is configured as a digital output
    *   The program enters the main loop

2.  **Main Loop (Continuous Blinking)**:
    *   **ON Phase**: The LED illuminates brightly
    *   **Wait**: The LED remains bright for exactly 2 seconds
    *   **OFF Phase**: The LED goes completely dark
    *   **Wait**: The LED remains dark for exactly 2 seconds
    *   **Repeat**: The cycle repeats indefinitely creating a steady 2-second ON, 2-second OFF pattern

3.  **Observable Behavior**:
    *   You will see the on-board LED blinking at a steady, slow pace
    *   Each complete cycle (ON + OFF) takes 4 seconds total
    *   The pattern continues until the Pico is powered off or reset

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin
import time

# Initialization: Create LED pin object configured as output
led = Pin(25, Pin.OUT)

# Main loop: Blink forever
while True:
    led.value(1)    # Turn LED ON (HIGH = 3.3V)
    time.sleep(2)   # Keep ON for 2 seconds
    led.value(0)    # Turn LED OFF (LOW = 0V)
    time.sleep(2)   # Keep OFF for 2 seconds
```

### 11. Common Mistakes

1.  **Wrong Pin Number for Pico W**: The Pico W uses a wireless LED that requires the string `"LED"` instead of pin number 25. Writing `Pin("LED", Pin.OUT)` is required for Pico W, but `Pin(25, Pin.OUT)` works for standard Pico. Using 25 on Pico W causes no visible LED blinking.

2.  **Forgetting to set Pin mode to OUTPUT**: Writing `led = Pin(25)` without specifying `Pin.OUT` may cause the LED to not light because the pin defaults to input mode. Always specify the pin direction: `Pin(25, Pin.OUT)`.

3.  **Confusing seconds with milliseconds**: Python's `time.sleep()` uses seconds as the unit. Writing `time.sleep(2000)` thinking it's 2000 milliseconds will actually pause for 2000 seconds (33 minutes). Always use seconds: `time.sleep(2)` for 2 seconds.

4.  **Using undefined variable**: Referencing `led.value(1)` before defining `led = Pin(25, Pin.OUT)` causes a `NameError: name 'led' is not defined` error. Always initialize variables before using them.

### 12. Try This Next

1.  **Fast Blink Mode**: Change both `time.sleep(2)` statements to `time.sleep(0.1)` to create a rapid 10Hz blinking effect. This demonstrates how timing affects perception.

2.  **Morse Code SOS**: Modify the pattern to blink the international distress signal: 3 short blinks (0.2s ON/OFF), 3 long blinks (0.6s ON/OFF), 3 short blinks. This teaches pattern sequencing.

3.  **Asymmetric Pattern**: Make the LED stay ON for 1 second but OFF for 3 seconds to create a "lighthouse" effect with longer dark periods between flashes.

4.  **Brightness Fading** (Advanced): Use PWM instead of digital ON/OFF to fade the LED in and out smoothly. This introduces analog output concepts using `PWM()` instead of `Pin()`.

5.  **Heartbeat Pattern**: Create a "double-thump" pattern: Blink twice quickly (0.15s ON, 0.15s OFF, 0.15s ON), then wait 1 second. This mimics a heartbeat rhythm.

## Project 0002: Blinking LED Patterns

### 2. Learning Objective
Create a non-uniform flashing pattern (Heartbeat) to understand complex timing sequences.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Asymmetric Delays** (Used in Step 8)
*   **Visual Rhythm** (Used in Step 8)
*   **Sequence Logic** (Used in Step 8)

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Red LED**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Red LED** | GP25 | On-board LED used for heartbeat (standard Pico) |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin to ...)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_wait`** (wait)
*   **from Variables, drag `set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   **from Text, drag `""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **led**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(25, Pin.OUT)` representing GP25 configured as digital output for the onboard red LED

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize LED Pin**:
    *   This project uses direct Python initialization for the onboard LED: `led = Pin(25, Pin.OUT)`
    *   In Blockly:
        *   From **Variables**, click **"Create variable..."** and name it **`led`**.
        *   Drag the **`set [led] to`** block.
        *   Assign it the value **`Pin(25, Pin.OUT)`**.
        *   **Snap** this block before the main loop in the initialization area.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** it below the initialization block.
3.  **Beat 1 - ON**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag a **`pico_gpio_write`** block.
    *   Set Pin to **`led`**.
    *   Set State to **HIGH (1)**.
    *   **Snap** this block inside the `pico_forever` loop.
4.  **Beat 1 - Wait**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag a **`pico_wait`** block.
    *   Set duration to **0.2** and unit to **seconds**.
    *   **Snap** below "Beat 1 - ON".
5.  **Beat 1 - OFF**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag another **`pico_gpio_write`** block.
    *   Set Pin to **`led`** and State to **LOW (0)**.
    *   **Snap** below "Beat 1 - Wait".
6.  **Gap - Wait**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag a **`pico_wait`** block.
    *   Set duration to **0.2** and unit to **seconds**.
    *   **Snap** below "Beat 1 - OFF".
7.  **Beat 2 - ON**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag a **`pico_gpio_write`** block.
    *   Set Pin to **`led`** and State to **HIGH (1)**.
    *   **Snap** below "Gap - Wait".
8.  **Beat 2 - Wait**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag a **`pico_wait`** block.
    *   Set duration to **0.2** and unit to **seconds**.
    *   **Snap** below "Beat 2 - ON".
9.  **Beat 2 - OFF**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag a **`pico_gpio_write`** block.
    *   Set Pin to **`led`** and State to **LOW (0)**.
    *   **Snap** below "Beat 2 - Wait".
10. **Rest - Wait**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag a **`pico_wait`** block.
    *   Set duration to **1.0** and unit to **seconds**.
    *   **Snap** below "Beat 2 - OFF".

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Loop**: Initiates infinite cycle.
2.  **Active**:
    *   Flash Pin 25 (0.2s)
    *   Gap (0.2s)
    *   Flash Pin 25 (0.2s)
    *   Rest (1.0s)
3.  **Repeat**: Simulates cardiac rhythm.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin
import time

# Initialization
led = Pin(25, Pin.OUT)

while True:
    # Beat 1
    led.value(1)
    time.sleep(0.2)
    led.value(0)
    time.sleep(0.2)
    
    # Beat 2
    led.value(1)
    time.sleep(0.2)
    led.value(0)
    
    # Rest
    time.sleep(1.0)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Even Delays**: Using `0.5s` for everything makes it just a fast blink, not a heartbeat. This loses the "rhythm" concept.
*   **Missing Off**: Forgetting to turn the LED off between beats, causing it to stay lit instead of pulsing.
*   **Wrong Pin Number**: Using a GPIO pin other than 25 (like 15) when the physical LED is connected to GP25.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Triple Beat**: Add a third flash for a high-stress heart rate by duplicating the ON/OFF/Wait blocks.
*   **Variable Speed**: Create a variable `rest_time` starting at `1.0` and decrease it by `0.1` after each loop to simulate a racing heart.
*   **Alert Signal**: Use an `if/else` block inside the loop to change the heartbeat speed only while a button is pressed.

## Project 0003: Manual LED Patterns Control

### 2. Learning Objective
Control a digital output using a digital input. Learn how to read the state of a button (High/Low) and map it directly to an LED throughout a polling loop.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Digital Input** (Used in Step 8)
*   **Polling Loop** (Used in Step 8)
*   **State Mapping** (Used in Step 8)
*   **Momentary Switch Logic** (Used in Step 8)
*   **Logic Blocks (if/else)** (Used in Step 8)

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Pushbutton**
*   **Green LED**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Button** | GP14 | Digital input with PULL_DOWN resistor enabled |
| **Green LED** | GP15 | Digital output, active HIGH |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_read`** (read Pin)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin to ...)
*   **from Logic & Math, drag `controls_if`** (if/else)
*   **from Logic & Math, drag `logic_compare`** ([A] [=] [B])

### 7. Variables
*   **btn**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)` representing GP14 configured as digital input with pull-down resistor for the pushbutton
*   **led**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(15, Pin.OUT)` representing GP15 configured as digital output for the green LED

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Button Pin**:
    *   From **Variables**, click **"Create variable..."** and name it **`btn`**.
    *   Drag the **`set [btn] to`** block.
    *   Set value to: **`Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
    *   **Snap** into the initialization area.
2.  **Initialize LED Pin**:
    *   From **Variables**, click **"Create variable..."** and name it **`led`**.
    *   Drag the **`set [led] to`** block.
    *   Set value to: **`Pin(15, Pin.OUT)`**.
    *   **Snap** below the `btn` initialization.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
3.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization section.
4.  **Check Input**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** block.
    *   Click the gear icon and drag an **else** branch into the `if` block.
    *   **Condition**: From **Logic & Math**, drag the **`[A] [=] [B]`** comparison block.
    *   **Left Side (A)**: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_read`**. Set Pin to **`btn`**.
    *   **Right Side (B)**: From **Logic & Math**, drag a numeric value block and set it to **1**.
    *   **Snap** the comparison into the `if` condition field.
5.  **Action (Pressed)**:
    *   Inside the **"do"** (if) part: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **`led`** and State to **HIGH (1)**.
6.  **Action (Released)**:
    *   Inside the **"else"** part: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **`led`** and State to **LOW (0)**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Poll**: Checks button state thousands of times per second.
2.  **True**: If button pressed, light goes ON.
3.  **False**: If released, light goes OFF.
4.  **Repeat**: Creates a responsive "Momentary" switch.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin

# Initialization
btn = Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
led = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)

while True:
    if btn.value() == 1:
        led.value(1) # Button Pressed -> LED ON
    else:
        led.value(0) # Button Released -> LED OFF
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Floating Pin**: Not enabling "PULL_DOWN" causes the button to read random noise when released, making the LED flicker.
*   **Reverse Logic**: Connecting the button to 3.3V vs GND changes whether the input is active-high or active-low.
*   **Missing Forever Loop**: Placing the `if` block outside a `pico_forever` loop causes the code to check the button once at startup and then stop.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Inverted Logic**: Modify the code so the LED is ON by default and turns OFF only when the button is held.
*   **Latched Switch**: Try to make the button work like a light switch (press to turn on, press again to turn off). Note: This requires a state variable.
*   **Input Speed Test**: Remove the `time.sleep` and see how fast the system reacts. Can you find a reason why some delay is still useful?

## Project 0004: LED Patterns Sequences

### 2. Learning Objective
Create a multi-step sequence simulating a Traffic Light (Green -> Yellow -> Red) to understand sequential logic.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **State Sequencing** (Used in Step 8)
*   **Multi-Pin Control** (Used in Step 8)
*   **Visual Signaling Standards** (Used in Step 8)
*   **Standard GPIO Control** (Used in Step 8)

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **3x LEDs (Red, Yellow, Green)**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Red LED** | GP15 | Safety/Stop Signal |
| **Yellow LED** | GP14 | Warning/Caution Signal |
| **Green LED** | GP13 | Proceed/Go Signal |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin to ...)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_wait`** (wait)
*   **from Variables, drag `set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   **from Text, drag `""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **red**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(15, Pin.OUT)` representing GP15 for the Red LED.
*   **yellow**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(14, Pin.OUT)` representing GP14 for the Yellow LED.
*   **green**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(13, Pin.OUT)` representing GP13 for the Green LED.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize LED Pins**:
    *   Create variables **`red`**, **`yellow`**, and **`green`**.
    *   For each, drag a **`set ... to`** block from **Variables**.
    *   Assign them values **`Pin(15, Pin.OUT)`**, **`Pin(14, Pin.OUT)`**, and **`Pin(13, Pin.OUT)`** respectively.
    *   **Snap** these blocks into the initialization area.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization blocks.
3.  **Green State (Go)**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag a **`pico_gpio_write`** block. Set Pin to **`green`** and State to **HIGH (1)**.
    *   Drag two more **`pico_gpio_write`** blocks. Set **`yellow`** to **LOW (0)** and **`red`** to **LOW (0)**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag a **`pico_wait`** block. Set duration to **3** and unit to **seconds**.
    *   **Snap** these into the `pico_forever` loop.
4.  **Yellow State (Caution)**:
    *   Drag three **`pico_gpio_write`** blocks. Set **`green`** to **LOW (0)**, **`yellow`** to **HIGH (1)**, and **`red`** to **LOW (0)**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag a **`pico_wait`** block. Set duration to **1** and unit to **second**.
    *   **Snap** below Green state.
5.  **Red State (Stop)**:
    *   Drag three **`pico_gpio_write`** blocks. Set **`green`** to **LOW (0)**, **`yellow`** to **LOW (0)**, and **`red`** to **HIGH (1)**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag a **`pico_wait`** block. Set duration to **3** and unit to **seconds**.
    *   **Snap** below Yellow state.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Green**: Traffic Flows (3s).
2.  **Yellow**: Warning (1s).
3.  **Red**: Stop (3s).
4.  **Repeat**.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin
import time

# Initialization
green = Pin(13, Pin.OUT)
yellow = Pin(14, Pin.OUT)
red = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)

while True:
    # Green (Go): 3 seconds
    green.value(1)
    yellow.value(0)
    red.value(0)
    time.sleep(3)
    
    # Yellow (Caution): 1 second
    green.value(0)
    yellow.value(1)
    red.value(0)
    time.sleep(1)
    
    # Red (Stop): 3 seconds
    green.value(0)
    yellow.value(0)
    red.value(1)
    time.sleep(3)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Ghost Light**: Forgetting to turn OFF the previous light (e.g., Green stays ON when Yellow turns ON).
*   **Wrong Pins**: Mixing up Yellow and Green wires.
*   **Rushed Timing**: Setting all delays too short (< 1s) makes the sequence unreadable.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **UK Mode**: Research the UK traffic light sequence and add a "Red + Yellow" state between Red and Green.
*   **Pedestrian Button**: Add a button that, when pressed, interrupts the current Green light and forces the sequence to Red.
*   **Maintenance Mode**: Use an `if/else` block and a light sensor to switch from the normal cycle to blinking Yellow (Maintenance Mode) when it gets dark.

## Project 0005: Interactive LED Patterns

### 2. Learning Objective
Learn how to implement conditional logic and state selection by controlling multiple outputs based on multiple digital inputs.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   Digital Input (Multiple)
*   Conditional Logic (`if-elseif-else`)
*   State Selection
*   Mutual Exclusion

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Red LED**
*   **Green LED**
*   **Button A**
*   **Button B**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Red LED** | GP15 | Mode A Indicator |
| **Green LED** | GP14 | Mode B Indicator |
| **Button A** | GP16 | Selection Button A (Pull-Down) |
| **Button B** | GP17 | Selection Button B (Pull-Down) |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Logic & Math, drag `controls_if`** (if / else if / else)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_read`** (read Pin)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin to ...)
*   **from Variables, drag `set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   **from Text, drag `""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **red**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(15, Pin.OUT)` representing GP15 for the Mode A indicator LED.
*   **green**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(14, Pin.OUT)` representing GP14 for the Mode B indicator LED.
*   **btnA**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(16, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)` representing GP16 for the Mode A selection button.
*   **btnB**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(17, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)` representing GP17 for the Mode B selection button.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Variables**:
    *   Create variables **`red`**, **`green`**, **`btnA`**, and **`btnB`**.
    *   For each, use the **`set ... to`** block from **Variables**.
    *   Assign values:
        *   `red` to `Pin(15, Pin.OUT)`
        *   `green` to `Pin(14, Pin.OUT)`
        *   `btnA` to `Pin(16, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`
        *   `btnB` to `Pin(17, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`
    *   **Snap** these into the initialization area at the top of your workspace.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization section.
3.  **Check Button A**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** block.
    *   Click the gear icon and drag an **else if** branch and an **else** branch into the `if` block.
    *   **If Condition**: From **Logic & Math**, drag the **`[A] [=] [B]`** comparison block.
    *   **Left Side (A)**: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_read`**. Set Pin to **`btnA`**.
    *   **Right Side (B)**: Set value to **1**.
    *   **Action**:
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set **`red`** to **HIGH (1)**.
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set **`green`** to **LOW (0)**.
4.  **Check Button B**:
    *   **Else If Condition**: From **Logic & Math**, drag another comparison. Read **`btnB`** and check if it equals **1**.
    *   **Action**:
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set **`red`** to **LOW (0)**.
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set **`green`** to **HIGH (1)**.
5.  **Default (Off)**:
    *   **Else (No buttons pressed)**:
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set **`red`** to **LOW (0)**.
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set **`green`** to **LOW (0)**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Poll**: Checks Button A.
2.  **Mode A**: If A pressed -> Red On.
3.  **Mode B**: If not A, check B. If B pressed -> Green On.
4.  **Off**: If neither -> All Off.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin

red = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)
green = Pin(14, Pin.OUT)
btnA = Pin(16, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
btnB = Pin(17, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)

while True:
    if btnA.value() == 1:
        red.value(1)
        green.value(0)
    elif btnB.value() == 1:
        red.value(0)
        green.value(1)
    else:
        red.value(0)
        green.value(0)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Priority Logic**: If you press BOTH buttons simultaneously, the Red LED will turn on because it is the first `if` condition checked. The Green LED logic is inside an `else if`, so it is only checked if Button A is NOT pressed.
*   **Floating Input**: Forgetting the `PULL_DOWN` parameter in the pin initialization can cause the LED to flicker randomly even when no button is pressed, as the pin "floats" between 0 and 1.
*   **Incomplete Else**: Not including the final `else` block to turn off LEDs will result in the last selected color staying on forever after the button is released.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Flash Mode**: Make "Mode A" blink the Red LED instead of just solid ON.
*   **3 Modes**: Use 2 switches for 4 combinations.
*   **Toggle**: Make the button act like a toggle switch (Click to change mode).

## Project 0006: Smart LED Patterns Switch

### 2. Learning Objective
Learn how to implement latching logic by converting a momentary pushbutton into a toggle switch (Click-On, Click-Off) using state variables and edge detection.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **State Variables**: Storing info (`isOn = True/False`).
*   **Edge Detection**: Reacting to the *change* (Press event).
*   **Debouncing**: Preventing multiple counts per press.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Pushbutton**
*   **Blue LED**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Button** | GP14 | Momentary pushbutton, active HIGH |
| **Blue LED** | GP15 | System state indicator |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Variables, drag `set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Logic & Math, drag `controls_if`** (if)
*   **from Logic & Math, drag `logic_boolean`** (true/false)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_read`** (read Pin)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin to ...)
*   **from Logic & Math, drag `logic_negate`** (not)
*   **from Logic & Math, drag `controls_whileUntil`** (repeat while)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_wait`** (wait)
*   **from Text, drag `""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)


### 7. Variables
*   **btn**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)` representing GP14 as digital input for the toggle button.
*   **led**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(15, Pin.OUT)` representing GP15 as digital output for the switchable LED.
*   **lightsOn**: Boolean variable used to track the toggle state (True = ON, False = OFF).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variables**:
    *   From **Variables**, click **"Create variable..."** and create **`btn`** and **`led`**.
    *   From **Variables**, drag the **`set [btn] to`** block. Set value to **`Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
    *   From **Variables**, drag the **`set [led] to`** block. Set value to **`Pin(15, Pin.OUT)`**.
    *   **Snap** these into the initialization area.
2.  **Initialize State Variable**:
    *   From **Variables**, click **"Create variable..."** and create **`lightsOn`**.
    *   From **Variables**, drag the **`set [lightsOn] to`** block. Set value to **False** (from Logic).
    *   **Snap** below the hardware initialization.
    *   The entire block group should be snapped into the **`start`** block.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
3.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization blocks.
4.  **Detect Press**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** block.
    *   **Condition**: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_read`**. Set Pin to **`btn`**.
    *   **Comparison**: Check if it equals **1** (using comparison block from **Logic & Math**).
5.  **Toggle Logic**:
    *   Inside the "If" block:
        *   From **Variables**, drag **`set [lightsOn] to`**.
        *   Value: From **Logic & Math**, drag the **`not`** block and connect the **`lightsOn`** variable block to it.
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **`led`** and State to the **`lightsOn`** variable block.
6.  **Debounce (Wait for Release)**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag the **`repeat while`** (`controls_whileUntil`) block.
    *   **Condition**: Repeat while **Smart IO** `pico_gpio_read` of **`btn`** is **1**.
    *   Place a small delay inside or immediately after to prevent CPU hogging.
    *   Finally, from **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set to **0.05** seconds for mechanical debounce.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Wait**: System waits for press.
2.  **Toggle**: Flips state variable (True->False).
3.  **Update**: Sets LED.
4.  **Hold**: Stalls until you release the button.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin
import time

btn = Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
led = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)
lightsOn = False

while True:
    if btn.value() == 1:
        lightsOn = not lightsOn # Toggle
        led.value(lightsOn)
        while btn.value() == 1: # Wait for release
            time.sleep(0.01)
        time.sleep(0.05) # Debounce
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Machine Gun Effect**: If the light flashes wildly while holding, you forgot the "Wait until released" loop.
*   **Logic Inversion**: Ensure the `not` logic is correctly applied to the state variable.
*   **Debounce Timing**: Skipping the `time.sleep(0.05)` may cause the button to toggle multiple times due to mechanical noise.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Double Click**: Can you make a double-click turn it off?
*   **Auto-Off**: If left on for 1 minute, turn it off automatically.
*   **Dimmer Toggle**: Instead of ON/OFF, make the toggle cycle through 3 brightness levels.

## Project 0007: LED Patterns Alarm System

### 2. Learning Objective
Understand how to implement high-speed strobing by alternating two LED outputs rapidly based on a digital input signal.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Strobing** (Used in Step 8): High-frequency switching (>5Hz).
*   **Nested Loops** (Used in Step 8): A loop (strobe) inside a logic block.
*   **Active Logic** (Used in Step 8): Doing something *while* a condition is true.
*   **Conditional Strobing** (Used in Step 8)

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Red LED**
*   **Blue LED**
*   **Pushbutton**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Red LED** | GP14 | Primary strobe indicator |
| **Blue LED** | GP15 | Secondary strobe indicator |
| **Button** | GP16 | Trigger button (Pull-Down) |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Logic & Math, drag `controls_if`** (if / else)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin to ...)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_wait`** (wait)
*   **from Variables, drag `set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   **from Text, drag `""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **red**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(14, Pin.OUT)` representing GP14 as digital output.
*   **blue**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(15, Pin.OUT)` representing GP15 as digital output.
*   **btn**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(16, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)` representing GP16 as digital input for the alarm trigger.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variables**:
    *   Create variables **`red`**, **`blue`**, and **`btn`**.
    *   From **Variables**, use **`set [red] to`** with value **`Pin(14, Pin.OUT)`**.
    *   From **Variables**, use **`set [blue] to`** with value **`Pin(15, Pin.OUT)`**.
    *   From **Variables**, use **`set [btn] to`** with value **`Pin(16, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
    *   **Snap** all blocks into the **`start`** (initialization) block.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** it below the initialization section.
3.  **Check Trigger**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** block.
    *   **Condition**: From **Logic & Math**, drag the **`[A] [=] [B]`** comparison block.
    *   **Left Side (A)**: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_read`**. Set Pin to **`btn`**.
    *   **Right Side (B)**: From **Logic & Math**, drag a numeric value block and set it to **1**.
    *   **Snap** the comparison into the `if` field.
4.  **Strobe Mode (If)**:
    *   Inside "If":
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_gpio_write`. Set `red` **HIGH**.
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_gpio_write`. Set `blue` **LOW**.
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_wait`. Set to **0.1s**.
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_gpio_write`. Set `red` **LOW**.
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_gpio_write`. Set `blue` **HIGH**.
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_wait`. Set to **0.1s**.
5.  **Idle Mode (Else)**:
    *   Inside "Else":
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_gpio_write`. Set `red` **LOW** (Ensure OFF).
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_gpio_write`. Set `blue` **LOW** (Ensure OFF).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Idle**: Button not pressed -> All Off.
2.  **Alarm**: Button held -> Flashes Red/Blue rapidly.
3.  **Release**: Immediately stops.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin
import time

red = Pin(14, Pin.OUT)
blue = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)
btn = Pin(16, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)

while True:
    if btn.value() == 1:
        red.value(1)
        blue.value(0)
        time.sleep(0.1)
        red.value(0)
        blue.value(1)
        time.sleep(0.1)
    else:
        red.value(0)
        blue.value(0)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Stuck On**: If you release exactly during Blue Phase, Blue stays on unless `Else` turns it off.
*   **Seizures**: Keep frequency reasonable; flashing too fast can be disruptive or invisible.
*   **Pin Mapping**: Swapping GP14 and GP15 in code while wiring GP16 to the button will lead to unexpected outputs.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Siren**: Add a buzzer that changes pitch with the lights.
*   **3-Color**: Add a White LED for a triple-strobe.
*   **Latching Alarm**: Modify the logic so the strobe keeps going even after the button is released, until a reset button is pressed.

## Project 0008: The LED Patterns Game

### 2. Learning Objective
Create a "Reflex Tester" game using randomness. The system waits for an unpredictable amount of time before flashing a light, testing the user's reaction speed.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Randomness** (Used in Step 8): Using the **Math** category for non-deterministic values.
*   **Unpredictability** (Used in Step 8): Non-deterministic intervals.
*   **Reaction Time** (Used in Step 8): Human limit testing.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **White LED**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LED** | GP15 | Target indicator for the reflex game |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Variables, drag `set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   **from Logic & Math, drag `math_random_float`** (random fraction)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin to ...)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_wait`** (wait)
*   **from Text, drag `""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **led**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(15, Pin.OUT)` representing GP15 for the game light.
*   **wait_time**: Float variable stores the random duration generated for the suspense phase.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variables**:
    *   From **Variables**, create **`led`** and **`wait_time`**.
    *   Assign **`led`** to **`Pin(15, Pin.OUT)`** using a set block.
    *   **Snap** into the initialization area.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization blocks.
3.  **Reset State**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set **`led`** to **LOW (0)**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set to **2.0 seconds**.
4.  **Suspense Phase (Random Delay)**:
    *   From **Variables**, drag **`set [wait_time] to`**.
    *   Value: From **Logic & Math**, drag **`random fraction`**.
        *   Combine with a multiplication block to scale the timing (e.g., `(random * 3) + 2` for a 2-5s range).
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **`wait_time`** and unit to **seconds**.
5.  **Action Phase (Target FLASH)**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set **`led`** to **HIGH (1)**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set to **0.5s**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set **`led`** to **LOW (0)**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Darkness**: System waits.
2.  **Random**: Computer picks a secret time (e.g., 3.4s).
3.  **Flash**: Light triggers.
4.  **Loop**: Repeats.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin
import time
import random

# Reaction game: wait 2 to 5 seconds randomly
led = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)

while True:
    led.value(0)
    
    # Suspense (2s to 5s)
    wait_time = random.uniform(2, 5)
    time.sleep(wait_time)
    
    # GO!
    led.value(1)
    time.sleep(0.5)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Predictable**: Without `random`, players just count the seconds.
*   **Too Fast**: If the "ON" time is set too low (e.g., 0.01s), the flicker might be missed by the human eye.
*   **Variable Scope**: Forgetting to update `wait_time` inside the loop will cause the same delay to repeat every round.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Reaction Timer**: Add a button. Measure the time from "Light ON" to "Button Press" (requires simple math: `end_time - start_time`).
*   **Higher Stakes**: Narrow the random range (e.g., 0.5s to 1.5s) to test extreme reflexes.
*   **Cheat Prevention**: Use another button. If the player presses *before* the light turns on, turn on a Red LED to indicate a "Foul".

## Project 0009: Automated LED Patterns

### 2. Learning Objective
Control an automatic night light system by reading an analog sensor (LDR).

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Analog Input (ADC)** (Used in Step 8): Reading values from 0-65535 (16-bit resolution).
*   **Threshold Comparison** (Used in Step 8): Deciding if X < Y.
*   **Feedback Loop** (Used in Step 8): Sensor -> Logic -> Actuator.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **LDR Sensor**
*   **LED**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LED** | GP15 | Indicator Light |
| **LDR** | GP26 (ADC0) | Light Dependent Resistor (Analog) |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Variables, drag `set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   **from Logic & Math, drag `controls_if`** (if / else)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_read`** (read Pin)
*   **from Logic & Math, drag `logic_compare`** (<)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin to ...)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_wait`** (wait)
*   **from Text, drag `""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **sensor**: ADC object initialized as `ADC(26)` representing GP26 (ADC0) for reading the LDR value.
*   **led**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(15, Pin.OUT)` representing GP15 for the indicator LED.
*   **val**: Numeric variable used to store the 16-bit analog reading from the sensor.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variables**:
    *   Create variables **`sensor`**, **`led`**, and **`val`**.
    *   From **Variables**, use **`set [sensor] to`** with value **`ADC(26)`**.
    *   From **Variables**, use **`set [led] to`** with value **`Pin(15, Pin.OUT)`**.
    *   **Snap** these into the initialization area at the top of your workspace.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization section.
3.  **Read Sensor**:
    *   From **Variables**, drag **`set [val] to`**.
    *   Value: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_read`**.
    *   In the **read** dropdown, select **Analog (u16)**.
    *   Set Pin to **`sensor`**.
4.  **Logic Check**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** (with else) block.
    *   **Condition**: From **Logic & Math**, drag the **`<`** comparison block.
    *   Compare **`val`** to the number **30000**.
5.  **Actions**:
    *   **If True (Dark)**:
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **`led`** and State to **HIGH (1)**.
    *   **Else (Light)**:
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **`led`** and State to **LOW (0)**.
6.  **Stabilize**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.1** seconds.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Sense**: Read Light Level (0-65535).
2.  **Compare**: Is it dark (< 30k)?
3.  **Act**: Turn LED On/Off.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import ADC, Pin
import time

sensor = ADC(26)
led = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)

while True:
    val = sensor.read_u16()
    if val < 30000: # Adjust threshold
        led.value(1)
    else:
        led.value(0)
    time.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Threshold Settings**: 30000 is a general threshold. You MUST verify your specific room's lighting by printing `val` to find the correct "dark" trigger point.
*   **LDR Pin**: Ensure the LDR is connected to one of the ADC pins (GP26, GP27, or GP28). Other pins will return 0 or 1 only.
*   **Wait Time**: Setting the wait time too high (e.g. 5s) will make the "Night Light" react too slowly to lighting changes.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Invert**: Make a burglar alarm that triggers on sudden *light* (Flashlight).
*   **Smart Dimmer**: Use PWM to make the LED brighter as it gets darker.
*   **Indicator Light**: Add a second LED that signals if the system is "Armed" vs "Disarmed" based on a button press.

## Project 0010: Mastering LED Patterns

### 2. Learning Objective
Learn how to control arrays of outputs algorithmically and utilize list comprehension to create the classic "KITT" / "Cylon" scanning effects.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Arrays/Lists**: `[16, 17, 18, 19, 20]`.
*   **List Comprehension**: Efficient creation.
*   **For Loops**: Iterating sequences.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **5x LEDs**
*   **5x Resistors**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LED 1** | GP16 | Left-most LED (Index 0) |
| **LED 2** | GP17 | Index 1 |
| **LED 3** | GP18 | Center LED (Index 2) |
| **LED 4** | GP19 | Index 3 |
| **LED 5** | GP20 | Right-most LED (Index 4) |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Lists, drag `lists_create_with`** (create list)
*   **from Logic & Math, drag `pico_for_item`** (for each item)
*   **from Logic & Math, drag `pico_for_range`** (count with i from 1 to 10)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin to ...)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_wait`** (wait)
*   **from Variables, drag `set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   **from Text, drag `""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **pins**: List of Pin numbers `[16, 17, 18, 19, 20]`.
*   **leds**: List of Pin objects initialized using list comprehension: `[Pin(p, Pin.OUT) for p in pins]`.
*   **led**: Iterator used for the "Sweep Right" loop.
*   **i**: Iterator used for the "Sweep Left" (Bounce-back) loop index.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Define Pin List**:
    *   From **Variables**, drag **`set [pins] to`**.
    *   From **Lists**, drag **`create_list_with`** and snap into the variable. 
    *   Add 5 items: **16, 17, 18, 19, 20**.
2.  **Initialize LEDs via Loop**:
    *   From **Loops**, drag **`pico_for_item`** (for each `p` in `pins`).
    *   **Action**: Inside the loop, create a Pin object for `p` (Output) and add it to the list **`leds`**.
    *   **Snap** these into the **`start`** block.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
3.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization blocks.
4.  **Sweep Right**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`for each item`**. Set variable to **`led`** and list to **`leds`**.
    *   **Action**: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set **`led`** to **HIGH (1)**.
    *   **Wait**: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set to **0.1s**.
    *   **Action**: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set **`led`** to **LOW (0)**.
5.  **Sweep Left (Bounce-back)**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`count with i from...`**. Set **`i`** from **3** down to **1** (click gear/arrow to change direction).
    *   **Action**: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set **`leds[i]`** to **HIGH (1)**.
    *   **Wait**: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set to **0.1s**.
    *   **Action**: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set **`leds[i]`** to **LOW (0)**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Setup**: Creates objects for all 5 LEDs.
2.  **Right**: 0->1->2->3->4.
3.  **Left**: 3->2->1 (Skips 4 and 0 to match).
4.  **Repeat**: Smooth wave.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin
import time

# Pin sequence: 16, 17, 18, 19, 20
leds = [Pin(p, Pin.OUT) for p in [16, 17, 18, 19, 20]]

while True:
    # Sweep Right
    for led in leds:
        led.value(1)
        time.sleep(0.1)
        led.value(0)
    
    # Sweep Left (excluding ends)
    for i in range(3, 0, -1):
        leds[i].value(1)
        time.sleep(0.1)
        leds[i].value(0)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Hardcoding**: Doing this with 20 blocks is painful. Use loops!
*   **Index Error**: `leds[5]` doesn't exist (indices are 0-4).
*   **Bounce**: Excluding endpoints creates a smoother visual bounce.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Comet Trail**: Modify the code so that the previous LED stays ON for a very short time after the next one turns ON, creating a "tail" effect.
*   **Adjustable Speed**: Connect a Potentiometer to GP26 and use the `pico_gpio_read` (Analog) value to set the `wait` duration.
*   **Expanding the Line**: Add 3 more LEDs and resistors. Notice how you only need to update the `pins` list in the initialization, and the rest of the code works automatically!

#  Batch 2: Button Logic 1

## Project 0011: Introduction to Button Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
Learn to read a digital input signal. You will program the Pico to listen for a button press and acknowledge it by printing a message to the computer screen, introducing the concept of Input vs Output.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Digital Input** (Used in Step 8): Measuring if voltage is High (3.3V) or Low (0V).
*   **Polling Loop** (Used in Step 8): Constantly checking a pin state in a loop.
*   **Edge Detection** (Used in Step 8): (Simplified) detecting the "Pressed" state.
*   **Console Output** (Used in Step 8): Using `print()` to debug via the computer.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Pushbutton**


### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Button** | GP14 | Connected with Internal Pull-Down |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`controls_if`** (if)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_read`** (read Pin)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_log`** (log/print)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`** (wait)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   From **Text**, drag **`""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **btn**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)` representing GP14 configured as digital input for the pushbutton.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Button Pin**:
    *   From **Variables**, click **"Create variable..."** and name it **`btn`**.
    *   Drag the **`set [btn] to`** block.
    *   Set value to: **`Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
    *   **Snap** into the initialization area.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization section.
3.  **Check Press**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** block.
    *   **Condition**: From **Logic & Math**, drag the **`[A] [=] [B]`** comparison block.
    *   **Left Side (A)**: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_read`**. Set Pin to **`btn`**.
    *   **Right Side (B)**: From **Logic & Math**, drag a numeric value block and set it to **1**.
    *   **Snap** into the `if` condition field.
4.  **Acknowledge (Action Phase)**:
    *   Inside the "do" (if) part:
        *   From the **Smart IO** category, drag **`pico_log`**.
        *   In the **log/print** field, snap a text string block from **Text** and enter **"Click!"**.
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.2** and unit to **seconds**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Poll**: The Pico checks "Is pin 14 High?".
2.  **Detect**: If you press, the answer is Yes.
3.  **React**: It prints "Click!" and waits briefly (debounce).

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin
import time

# Initialization
btn = Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)

while True:
    if btn.value():
        print("Click!")
        time.sleep(0.2) # Debounce delay to prevent multiple prints per click
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Spamming**: If you see "Click! Click! Click!" continuously when NOT pressing, your button might be wired to 3.3V permanently, or you forgot the Pull-Down resistor setting.
*   **No Text**: Make sure your "Serial Console" or "Repl" window is open to see the output.
*   **Wait Time**: Setting the wait time too high (e.g., 2.0s) will make the Pico ignore subsequent clicks for too long.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Counter**: Create a variable `count`. Increment it every click. Print "Click #1", "Click #2".
*   **Release**: Print "Pressed" when down, and "Released" when you let go.
*   **LED Response**: Connect an LED and make it blink exactly once when the button is clicked.

## Project 0012: Blinking Button Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
Control a digital output using a button input. Create a device that changes its blink speed based on user input (Pressed = Fast, Released = Slow).

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Variable Timing** (Used in Step 8): Changing `sleep` duration dynamically.
*   **State-Dependent Behavior** (Used in Step 8): Acting different based on input.
*   **Blocking Code** (Used in Step 8): Understanding how `wait` pauses everything, including button checks.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Pushbutton**
*   **Red LED**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Button** | GP14 | Connected with Internal Pull-Down |
| **LED** | GP15 | Indicator Output |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`controls_if`** (if / else)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin to ...)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_read`** (read Pin)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`** (wait)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   From **Text**, drag **`""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **led**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(15, Pin.OUT)` representing GP15 for the red indicator LED.
*   **btn**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)` representing GP14 for the speed selection button.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variables**:
    *   Create variables **`led`** and **`btn`**.
    *   From **Variables**, use **`set [led] to`** with value **`Pin(15, Pin.OUT)`**.
    *   From **Variables**, use **`set [btn] to`** with value **`Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
    *   **Snap** into the initialization area (before the loop).

**B. Main Loop Phase**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization section.
3.  **LED On Phase**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **`led`** and State to **HIGH (1)**.
4.  **Timing Selection (On Wait)**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** (with else) block.
    *   **Condition**: Check if **`pico_gpio_read`** for **`btn`** equals **1**.
    *   **If (Pressed)**: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.1** seconds.
    *   **Else (Released)**: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **1.0** seconds.
5.  **LED Off Phase**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **`led`** and State to **LOW (0)**.
6.  **Timing Selection (Off Wait)**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`controls_if`** (with else).
    *   **Condition**: Check if **`pico_gpio_read`** for **`btn`** equals **1**.
    *   **If (Pressed)**: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.1** seconds.
    *   **Else (Released)**: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **1.0** seconds.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **On**: Light Turns On.
2.  **Check**: Pressed? Wait short. Released? Wait long.
3.  **Off**: Light Turns Off.
4.  **Check**: Repeat wait logic.
5.  **Note**: Because of the long 1s waits, the button may feel unresponsive until the current wait cycle finishes.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin
import time

# Initialization
led = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)
btn = Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)

while True:
    led.value(1)
    if btn.value():
        time.sleep(0.1)
    else:
        # Long wait blocks the loop; button input is ignored during this time
        time.sleep(1.0)
        
    led.value(0)
    if btn.value():
        time.sleep(0.1)
    else:
        time.sleep(1.0)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Laggy Response**: Because we wait *inside* the loop, if you press the button during the 1-second "Slow Wait", it won't speed up until that 1 second finishes.
*   **Wait Synchronization**: Ensure you change the timing in both the "LED ON" and "LED OFF" selection blocks, otherwise paths will blink with uneven durations.
*   **Blocking Logic**: Understanding that `time.sleep` (wait) stops all other code execution is key. The Pico cannot "see" the button while it is in the middle of a 1-second wait.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Hyper Speed**: Make the fast blink 0.01s (almost solid on).
*   **Reverse Logic**: Make the LED slow down (1.0s) when the button is pressed and speed up (0.1s) when released.
*   **Fading Fast**: Instead of just changing speed, use a variable for the wait time and decrease it every time the button is clicked to make the blinking get faster and faster.

## Project 0013: Manual Button Logic Control (Dead Man's Switch)

### 2. Learning Objective
Build a safety system that requires constant human presence. Create a device that alarms when a button is released, mimicking train or machinery safety switches.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Fail-Safe**: Designing system defaults to be safe (or alarm) on failure.
*   **Inverted Logic**: Active = OK, Inactive = Danger.
*   **Timers**: Resetting a countdown.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Pushbutton**
*   **Green LED, Red LED**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Button** | GP14 | Safety switch (requires constant contact) |
| **Green LED** | GP15 | "System OK" indicator |
| **Red LED** | GP16 | "ALARM" indicator |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`controls_if`** (if / else)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin to ...)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_read`** (read Pin)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`** (wait)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`logic_negate`** (not)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   From **Text**, drag **`""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **btn**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)` representing GP14 for the safety switch.
*   **green**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(15, Pin.OUT)` representing GP15 for the safety indicator.
*   **red**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(16, Pin.OUT)` representing GP16 for the alarm indicator.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variables**:
    *   Create variables **`btn`**, **`green`**, and **`red`**.
    *   Set **`btn`** to **`Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
    *   Set **`green`** to **`Pin(15, Pin.OUT)`**.
    *   Set **`red`** to **`Pin(16, Pin.OUT)`**.
    *   **Snap** these into the initialization area (before the loop).

**B. Main Loop Phase**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization section.
3.  **Active Check**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** (with else) block.
    *   **Condition**: Check if **`pico_gpio_read`** for **`btn`** equals **1**.
4.  **Safe Mode (Pressed)**:
    *   Inside the **"do"** (if) part: 
        *   Set **`green`** to **HIGH (1)**.
        *   Set **`red`** to **LOW (0)**.
5.  **Grace Period (Released)**:
    *   Inside the **"else"** part:
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **1.0** second.
        *   From **Logic & Math**, drag another **`controls_if`** block.
        *   **Condition**: Check if **`pico_gpio_read`** for **`btn`** equals **0** (Still released).
        *   **Action**: Set **`green`** to **LOW (0)** and **`red`** to **HIGH (1)**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Held**: Green Light (Safe).
2.  **Released**: Wait 1s (Grace Period).
3.  **Still Released**: Red Light (Alarm).

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin
import time

btn = Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
green = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)
red = Pin(16, Pin.OUT)

while True:
    if btn.value():
        green.value(1)
        red.value(0)
    else:
        # Grace period
        time.sleep(1)
        if not btn.value(): # Check again
            green.value(0)
            red.value(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Instant Alarm**: Dealing with the logic to add the "1 Second Delay" can be tricky. Beginners often make it alarm immediately upon release by omitting the nested check.
*   **Flicker**: Ensure the Red light doesn't blip off every loop cycle by properly using the `else` logic.
*   **Wait Blocking**: Remember that while the Pico is in the 1-second "Grace Period" sleep, it cannot detect if the button is pressed again until the sleep finishes.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Latching Alarm**: Make it require a separate "Reset Button" to turn off the Red Alarm once triggered, even if the main button is pressed again.
*   **Buzzer Phase**: Add a piezzo buzzer that chirps during the 1-second grace period before going full alarm.
*   **Multi-Switch**: Require TWO buttons (two hands on switches) to maintain Safe Mode.

## Project 0014: Button Logic Sequences (Secret Code)

### 2. Learning Objective
Create a combination lock using state tracking. You will build a system that requires "A then B then A" button presses to unlock, introducing the concept of State Machines.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **State Tracking** (Used in Step 8): "I have seen the first button, waiting for second."
*   **Sequence Validation** (Used in Step 8): Checking order.
*   **Reset on Error** (Used in Step 8): If wrong button, start over.
*   **Finite State Machine** (Used in Step 8): System logic based on distinct stages.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Button A, Button B**
*   **Green LED, Red LED**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Button A** | GP10 | First input |
| **Button B** | GP11 | Second input |
| **Reset** | GP14 | Optional Reset Pin |
| **Green LED** | GP15 | Unlock Indicator |
| **Red LED** | GP16 | Error Indicator |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`controls_if`** (if / else if / else)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`[A] [=] [B]`** (comparison)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_read`** (read Pin)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin to ...)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`** (wait)
*   From **Text**, drag **`""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **btnA**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(10, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)` representing GP10 for Button A (First Input).
*   **btnB**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(11, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)` representing GP11 for Button B (Second Input).
*   **green**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(15, Pin.OUT)` representing GP15 for the "Success" indicator.
*   **red**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(16, Pin.OUT)` representing GP16 for the "Error" indicator.
*   **stage**: Number variable used to track the progress of the secret sequence (0 = Start, 3 = UNLOCKED).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variables**:
    *   Create variables **`btnA`**, **`btnB`**, **`green`**, and **`red`**.
    *   Set **`btnA`** to **`Pin(10, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
    *   Set **`btnB`** to **`Pin(11, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
    *   Set **`green`** to **`Pin(15, Pin.OUT)`**.
    *   Set **`red`** to **`Pin(16, Pin.OUT)`**.
2.  **Initialize Sequence Variable**:
    *   Create variable **`stage`**. Set it to **0**.
    *   **Snap** all blocks into the **`start`** (initialization) block.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
3.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization blocks.
4.  **Check Sequence Status**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** block.
    *   **Condition**: Check if **`stage`** equals **3**.
    *   **Action**: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **15** (green) and State to **HIGH (1)**.
5.  **Button A Logic**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** block.
    *   **Condition**: Check if **`pico_gpio_read`** of **`btnA`** equals **1**.
    *   **Action (Nested Ifs)**:
        *   If **`stage`** equals **0**: Set **`stage`** to **1**.
        *   Else if **`stage`** equals **2**: Set **`stage`** to **3**.
        *   Else: Set **`stage`** to **0** and blink the **`red`** LED (GP16).
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.3** seconds.
6.  **Button B Logic**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** block.
    *   **Condition**: Check if **`pico_gpio_read`** of **`btnB`** equals **1**.
    *   **Action (Nested Ifs)**:
        *   If **`stage`** equals **1**: Set **`stage`** to **2**.
        *   Else: Set **`stage`** to **0** and blink the **`red`** LED (GP16).
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.3** seconds.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Stage 0 (Locked).
2.  **Press A**: Moves to Stage 1.
3.  **Press B**: Moves to Stage 2.
4.  **Press A**: Moves to Stage 3 (Unlock/Win).
5.  **Wrong Move**: Triggers Red Blink and immediately Resets to Stage 0.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin
import time

# Initialization
btnA = Pin(10, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
btnB = Pin(11, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
green = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)
red = Pin(16, Pin.OUT)
stage = 0

while True:
    if stage == 3:
        green.value(1) # Unlocked (Stage 3)
        
    if btnA.value():
        if stage == 0: stage = 1 # Correct start
        elif stage == 2: stage = 3 # Correct finish
        else: 
            stage = 0 # Wrong sequence: Reset
            red.value(1); time.sleep(0.5); red.value(0)
        time.sleep(0.3) # Debounce
        
    if btnB.value():
        if stage == 1: stage = 2 # Correct middle
        else: 
            stage = 0 # Wrong sequence: Reset
            red.value(1); time.sleep(0.5); red.value(0)
        time.sleep(0.3) # Debounce
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Bouncing**: One press might be read as two interactions, skipping a stage. `time.sleep(0.3)` is crucial after every press for stable detection.
*   **Missing Reset**: Forgetting to reset `stage` to 0 on a wrong button press makes the lock impossible to open until a power cycle.
*   **Wait Timing**: If the debounce wait is too long (e.g., 1.0s), the buttons will feel sluggish and user might miss the "Double Click" window.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Long Code**: Require a 5-step sequence (e.g., A-A-B-A-B) for a higher security level.
*   **Timeout**: If the user waits more than 5 seconds between presses, reset the `stage` to 0 automatically.
*   **Buzzer Feedback**: Add a short "Beep" for every correct button press and a long "Buzz" for an incorrect one.

## Project 0015: Interactive Button Logic (Voting Machine)

### 2. Learning Objective
Build a voting counter system. Create a tally tracker that counts two separate values ("Cats" vs "Dogs") and reports the score.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Incrementing** (Used in Step 8): `x = x + 1`.
*   **Data Storage** (Used in Step 8): Variables hold values in RAM.
*   **String Formatting** (Used in Step 8): Combining text and numbers.
*   **Volatile Memory** (Used in Step 8): Data is lost when power is removed.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **2 Buttons**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Button A** | GP10 | Vote for "Cats" |
| **Button B** | GP11 | Vote for "Dogs" |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`change [variable] by 1`** (math_change)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`controls_if`** (if)
*   From **Text**, drag **`create text with`** (text_join)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_log`** (log/print)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_read`** (read Pin)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`** (wait)
*   From **Text**, drag **`""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **btnA**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(10, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)` representing GP10 for Cat voting.
*   **btnB**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(11, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)` representing GP11 for Dog voting.
*   **cats**: Number variable used to track the cumulative count of Cat votes.
*   **dogs**: Number variable used to track the cumulative count of Dog votes.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variables**:
    *   Create variables **`btnA`** and **`btnB`**.
    *   Set **`btnA`** to **`Pin(10, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
    *   Set **`btnB`** to **`Pin(11, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
2.  **Initialize Vote Counters**:
    *   Create variables **`cats`** and **`dogs`**.
    *   Set **`cats`** to **0**.
    *   Set **`dogs`** to **0**.
    *   **Snap** all initialization blocks into the **`start`** block.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
3.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization section.
4.  **Count Cats**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** block.
    *   **Condition**: Check if **`pico_gpio_read`** for **`btnA`** equals **1**.
    *   **Action**: 
        *   From **Variables**, use **`change [cats] by 1`**.
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_log`**.
        *   Using **`create text with`** (from **Text**), report the score: `"Cats: "`, the variable **`cats`**, `" Dogs: "`, and the variable **`dogs`**.
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.3** seconds.
5.  **Count Dogs**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag another **`controls_if`** block.
    *   **Condition**: Check if **`pico_gpio_read`** for **`btnB`** equals **1**.
    *   **Action**: 
        *   From **Variables**, use **`change [dogs] by 1`**.
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_log`**.
        *   Using **`create text with`** (from **Text**), report the updated score.
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.3** seconds.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Idle**: Waits for input in the forever loop.
2.  **Vote**: Identify which button was pressed and increment the corresponding variable.
3.  **Report**: Print new totals to the serial console.
4.  **Debounce**: A 0.3s wait is critical to prevent a single physical press from being counted multiple times as the switch "bounces".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin
import time

# Initialization
btnA = Pin(10, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
btnB = Pin(11, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
cats = 0
dogs = 0

while True:
    if btnA.value():
        cats += 1
        print(f"Cats: {cats}, Dogs: {dogs}")
        time.sleep(0.3) # Software debounce
    if btnB.value():
        dogs += 1
        print(f"Cats: {cats}, Dogs: {dogs}")
        time.sleep(0.3) # Software debounce

# Note: cats and dogs variables are in RAM. Data resets on power loss (Volatile).
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Double Counting**: If score jumps by 2 or more per click, increase the debounce delay (`0.3s` is usually minimum).
*   **Lost Data**: This system is "volatile"—turning off the power or resetting the Pico will wipe all votes back to zero.
*   **Wait Blocking**: If you press the second button exactly while the first button's 0.3s debounce wait is active, that second vote will be ignored.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Reset Button**: Add a third button that resets both `cats` and `dogs` back to 0.
*   **Victory LED**: If `cats` reaches 10, turn on a Green LED to signal victory.
*   **Percentage**: Calculate the percentage of votes for each side using the total (`cats + dogs`).

## Project 0016: Smart Button Logic Switch (3-Way)

### 2. Learning Objective
Build a multi-state switch with variable brightness. Create a 3-way dimmer (Off -> Low -> High -> Off) controlled by a single button.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **State Cycling** (Used in Step 8): Moving sequentially through a list (0, 1, 2, 0...).
*   **Modulo Logic** (Used in Step 8): Using math to reset counters efficiently.
*   **PWM Dimming** (Used in Step 8): Adjusting brightness levels via duty cycle.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Pushbutton**
*   **LED**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Button** | GP14 | Internal Pull-Down |
| **LED** | GP15 | Configured as PWM Output |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`controls_if`** (if / else if / else)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`change [variable] by 1`** (math_change)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`remainder of [A] / [B]`** (modulo)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_pwm`** (pulse Pin ...)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_read`** (read Pin)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`** (wait)
*   From **Text**, drag **`""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **btn**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)` representing GP14 for input control.
*   **led**: PWM object initialized as `PWM(Pin(15))` representing GP15 for dimmable output.
*   **mode**: Number variable used to track the cycle state (0 = Off, 1 = Low, 2 = High).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variables**:
    *   Create variables **`btn`** and **`led`**.
    *   Set **`btn`** to **`Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
    *   Set **`led`** to **`PWM(Pin(15))`** (from Smart IO PWM section).
2.  **Initialize Mode**:
    *   Create variable **`mode`**. Set it to **0**.
    *   **Snap** all initialization blocks into the **`start`** (initialization) block.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
3.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization section.
4.  **Detect Click**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** block.
    *   **Condition**: Check if **`pico_gpio_read`** for **`btn`** equals **1**.
    *   **Action**: 
        *   From **Variables**, use **`change [mode] by 1`**. 
        *   From **Variables**, drag **`set [mode] to`**.
        *   Value: From **Logic & Math**, drag the **`remainder of`** block. Set it to **`mode`** / **3**.
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.3** seconds.
5.  **Update Brightness Phase**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** block. Click the gear to add `else if` and `else` branches.
    *   **Branch 1 (Off)**: If **`mode`** equals **0**. Use **`pico_pwm`**. Set Pin to **15** and duty to **0**.
    *   **Branch 2 (Low)**: Else If **`mode`** equals **1**. Use **`pico_pwm`**. Set Pin to **15** and duty to **13000**.
    *   **Branch 3 (High)**: Else. Use **`pico_pwm`**. Set Pin to **15** and duty to **65535**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Idle**: Keeps old brightness.
2.  **Click**: Increments mode (0->1->2->0).
3.  **Update**: Sets PWM duty cycle.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin, PWM
import time

# Initialization
btn = Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
led = PWM(Pin(15))
led.freq(1000)
mode = 0 # 0=Off, 1=Low, 2=High

while True:
    if btn.value():
        mode = (mode + 1) % 3 # Cycle through 0, 1, 2
        time.sleep(0.2) # Debounce
        
    # Apply brightness via PWM duty cycle (0-65535)
    if mode == 0: led.duty_u16(0)
    if mode == 1: led.duty_u16(13000)
    if mode == 2: led.duty_u16(65535)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Fast Cycling**: Without the `Wait 0.3s` debounce, one press cycles through all modes instantly due to mechanical contact noise, likely landing back on "Off".
*   **PWM Range**: Entering values above 65535 for the PWM duty cycle will cause an error in MicroPython, as it expects a 16-bit unsigned integer.
*   **Missing Modulo**: Forgetting the modulo operation will cause the `mode` variable to increase indefinitely (3, 4, 5...), which will result in the LED staying on "High" (the `else` branch) forever.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **More Steps**: Increase the modulo to 4 (`mode % 4`) and add a "Medium" brightness level (e.g., 30000 duty cycle).
*   **Manual Reset**: Add a second button that directly sets `mode` to 0 (Off) regardless of the current state.
*   **Visual Indicator**: Add a different colored LED for each mode (e.g., Green for Low, Red for High) to signal the current state to the user.
