## Project 0017: Button Logic Alarm System (Panic Button)

### 2. Learning Objective
Create a panic alarm with hold-duration detection. Build a system that triggers only if a button is held for 3 seconds, prohibiting accidental activation.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Time Measurement** (Used in Step 8): Counting seconds while a condition is true.
*   **Hold Logic** (Used in Step 8): Actions requiring sustained input to prevent accidental triggers.
*   **Blocking Loops** (Used in Step 8): Freezing code execution intentionally.
*   **Non-recoverable States** (Used in Step 8): Logic that cannot be exited without a reset.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Pushbutton**
*   **Red LED**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Button** | GP14 | Internal Pull-Down required for panic trigger |
| **LED** | GP15 | Alarm Indicator |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`repeat while`** (controls_whileUntil)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`controls_if`** (if)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`change [variable] by 0.1`** (math_change)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_read`** (read Pin)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin to ...)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`** (wait)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`true`** (logic_boolean)
*   From **Text**, drag **`""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **btn**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)` representing GP14 for the panic button.
*   **led**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(15, Pin.OUT)` representing GP15 for the alarm indicator.
*   **holdTime**: Number variable used to track how many seconds the button has been held.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variables**:
    *   Create variables **`btn`** and **`led`**.
    *   Set **`btn`** to **`Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
    *   Set **`led`** to **`Pin(15, Pin.OUT)`**.
2.  **Initialize Hold Counter**:
    *   Create variable **`holdTime`**. Set it to **0**.
    *   **Snap** all initialization blocks into the **`start`** block.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
3.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization section.
4.  **Detect Touch**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** block.
    *   **Condition**: Check if **`pico_gpio_read`** for **`btn`** equals **1**.
    *   **Action (Reset Timer)**: From **Variables**, drag **`set [holdTime] to`** 0.
5.  **Check Sustain While Holding**:
    *   Inside the "do" (if) part of Step 4:
        *   From **Logic & Math**, drag the **`repeat while`** block.
        *   **Condition**: Check if **`pico_gpio_read`** for **`btn`** equals **1**.
        *   **Action**: 
            *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.1** seconds.
            *   From **Variables**, drag **`change [holdTime] by 0.1`**.
            *   From **Logic & Math**, drag another **`controls_if`** block.
            *   **Condition**: Check if **`holdTime`** is greater than or equal to **3.0**.
            *   **Action**: Move to the strobe sequence (Step 6).
6.  **Terminal Alarm Phase**:
    *   Inside the nested `if` (holdTime >= 3.0):
        *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`repeat while`** block.
        *   **Condition**: Snap the **`true`** block from **Logic & Math**.
        *   **Action (Strobe)**: 
            *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **15** and State to **HIGH (1)**.
            *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set to **0.1s**.
            *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **15** and State to **LOW (0)**.
            *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set to **0.1s**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Touch**: System starts counting. "0.1... 0.2...".
2.  **Release Early**: System stops counting. Nothing happens.
3.  **Hold 3s**: System hits limit. Enters "Alarm Mode" (Infinite blinking). Restart required to stop.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin
import time

# Initialization
btn = Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
led = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)
holdTime = 0

while True:
    if btn.value():
        holdTime = 0
        # Sustain check
        while btn.value():
            time.sleep(0.1)
            holdTime += 0.1
            if holdTime >= 3.0:
                # Terminal Alarm Mode (Requires hardware reset / power cycle)
                while True:
                    led.value(1)
                    time.sleep(0.1)
                    led.value(0)
                    time.sleep(0.1)
    time.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Impatience**: 3 seconds feels longer than you think! Users often release just early enough to miss the trigger.
*   **Power Cycle Required**: This project intentionally enters a terminal `while True` loop once triggered. You must reset the Pico to run the code again unless you add a reset button.
*   **Blocking Main Loop**: While the `while` loop is counting for 3 seconds, the rest of the program is "stuck". This is intentional here, but usually, you'd want non-blocking timers for complex apps.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Warning Flash**: Make the LED blink slowly after 1.5 seconds of holding to warn the user they are approaching the panic limit.
*   **Silent Alarm**: Send a USB serial message ("PANIC TRIGGERED") instead of a light for a covert notification.
*   **Reset Key**: Add a second button on GP16 that breaks the terminal loop and resets the alarm.

## Project 0018: The Button Logic Game (Fastest Finger)

### 2. Learning Objective
Build a competitive reaction game. Create a "fastest finger first" system that determines which of two buttons was pressed first, locking out the loser.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Race Conditions** (Used in Step 8): Determining which input arrives first.
*   **Lockout Logic** (Used in Step 8): Ignoring all further inputs after a winner is declared.
*   **Fairness** (Used in Step 8): Polling inputs effectively in a high-speed loop.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Button A, Button B**
*   **LED A, LED B**
*   **Start LED**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Button A** | GP10 | Player 1 Input |
| **Button B** | GP11 | Player 2 Input |
| **LED A** | GP14 | Player 1 Win Indicator |
| **LED B** | GP15 | Player 2 Win Indicator |
| **Start LED** | GP25 | On-board LED for "GO" signal |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`break`** (controls_flow_statements)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`controls_if`** (if)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin ...)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_read`** (read Pin)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`random integer from 1 to 100`** (math_random_int)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`** (wait)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`repeat while`** (controls_whileUntil)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   From **Text**, drag **`""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **btnA**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(10, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)` representing GP10 for Player 1.
*   **btnB**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(11, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)` representing GP11 for Player 2.
*   **ledA**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(14, Pin.OUT)` representing GP14 for Player 1 victory light.
*   **ledB**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(15, Pin.OUT)` representing GP15 for Player 2 victory light.
*   **start**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(25, Pin.OUT)` representing the on-board signal LED.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variables**:
    *   Create variables **`btnA`**, **`btnB`**, **`ledA`**, **`ledB`**, and **`start`**.
    *   Set **`btnA`** to **`Pin(10, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
    *   Set **`btnB`** to **`Pin(11, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
    *   Set **`ledA`** to **`Pin(14, Pin.OUT)`**.
    *   Set **`ledB`** to **`Pin(15, Pin.OUT)`**.
    *   Set **`start`** to **`Pin(25, Pin.OUT)`**.
    *   **Snap** into the **`start`** initialization block.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
2.  **Reset Game**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pins **14** (ledA), **15** (ledB), and **25** (start) to **LOW (0)**.
3.  **Suspense**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**.
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`random integer`**. Set range from **2** to **5**.
    *   **Snap** into the wait duration field.
4.  **GO Signal**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin **25** (start) to **HIGH (1)**.
5.  **The Race (Lockout logic)**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`repeat while`** block.
    *   **Condition**: Snap the **`true`** boolean block from **Logic & Math**.
    *   **Action**:
        *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** block.
        *   **Condition**: Check if **`pico_gpio_read`** of **`btnA`** equals **1**.
        *   **Action (Winner A)**: 
            *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin **14** (ledA) to **HIGH (1)**.
            *   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`break`** to exit the lockout loop.
        *   Below the first `if`, drag another **`controls_if`** block.
        *   **Condition**: Check if **`pico_gpio_read`** of **`btnB`** equals **1**.
        *   **Action (Winner B)**: 
            *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin **15** (ledB) to **HIGH (1)**.
            *   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`break`**.
6.  **Victory Display**:
    *   Below the lockout loop: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **4** seconds. (The program then restarts the main loop).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Wait**: Random delay.
2.  **Start**: LED Lights up.
3.  **Race**: Code checks A, then B constantly.
4.  **Win**: First detected press lights their LED and *Breaks* the race loop, ignoring the other.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin
import time
import random

btnA = Pin(10, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
btnB = Pin(11, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
ledA = Pin(14, Pin.OUT)
ledB = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)
start = Pin(25, Pin.OUT)

while True:
    ledA.value(0); ledB.value(0); start.value(0)
    time.sleep(random.uniform(2,5))
    start.value(1)
    
    while True:
        if btnA.value():
            ledA.value(1)
            break
        if btnB.value():
            ledB.value(1)
            break
    time.sleep(4) # Show winner
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Cheating**: Holding the button down before the light triggers guarantees a win. 
*   **False Start**: This version does not penalize jumping the gun. In professional "Fastest Finger" systems, a pre-GO press results in a lockout or foul.
*   **Order Bias**: Player A is checked slightly before B. At MicroPython speeds (~MHz), this difference is millions of times faster than human reaction.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **False Start Penalty**: If button pressed *while* Start LED is off -> The player is disqualified or the opponent wins immediately.
*   **Scoreboard**: Keep a tally of wins in variables `scoreA` and `scoreB` and print them to the console.
*   **Best of Three**: Create a loop that runs 3 times and declares an overall match winner.

## Project 0019: Automated Button Logic (Staircase Timer)

### 2. Learning Objective
Create a re-triggerable timer. Build a light suitable for a staircase or hallway that stays on for a set time, but extends that time if the button is pressed again.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Re-triggering** (Used in Step 8): Resetting a countdown timer to its maximum value before it expires.
*   **Countdown Loop** (Used in Step 8): Using a variable to track and decrement time.
*   **Smart Automation** (Used in Step 8): Designing systems that turn off after inactivity.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Pushbutton**
*   **LED**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Button** | GP14 | Timer Trigger (Pull-Down) |
| **LED** | GP15 | Timed Indicator |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`change [variable] by -0.1`** (math_change)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`controls_if`** (if / else)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`[A] [>] [B]`** (comparison)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_read`** (read Pin)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin to ...)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`** (wait)
*   From **Text**, drag **`""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **btn**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)` representing GP14 for the timer trigger.
*   **led**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(15, Pin.OUT)` representing GP15 for the timed light.
*   **timeLeft**: Number variable used to store the remaining active time in seconds.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variables**:
    *   Create variables **`btn`** and **`led`**.
    *   Set **`btn`** to **`Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
    *   Set **`led`** to **`Pin(15, Pin.OUT)`**.
2.  **Initialize State**:
    *   Create variable **`timeLeft`**. Set it to **0**.
    *   **Snap** all initialization blocks into the **`start`** block.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
3.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization section.
4.  **Trigger Logic**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** block.
    *   **Condition**: Check if **`pico_gpio_read`** for **`btn`** equals **1**.
    *   **Action**: From **Variables**, drag **`set [timeLeft] to`** 10.
5.  **Timer Decay Phase**:
    *   Below the trigger logic: From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** block with an **else** branch.
    *   **Condition**: From **Logic & Math**, drag the **`>`** comparison block. Check if variable **`timeLeft`** is greater than **0**.
    *   **Action (On & Decay)**: 
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **15** (led) and State to **HIGH (1)**.
        *   From **Variables**, drag **`change [timeLeft] by -0.1`**.
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.1** seconds.
    *   **Else (Expiry)**:
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **15** (led) and State to **LOW (0)**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Idle**: Light off.
2.  **Press**: Timer set to 10. Light On.
3.  **Loop**: Timer decreases by 0.1 every 0.1s.
4.  **Re-Press**: Timer jumps back to 10 (extending light).

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin
import time

# Initialization
btn = Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
led = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)
timeLeft = 0

while True:
    if btn.value():
        timeLeft = 10 # Reset timer to full duration (Re-trigger)
        
    if timeLeft > 0:
        led.value(1)
        timeLeft -= 0.1 # Small decrements for responsiveness
        time.sleep(0.1) # Prevents blocking button checks for long periods
    else:
        led.value(0)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Blocking Logic**: If you used a single `Wait 10s` block instead of a loop, the system would become "deaf" to the button while waiting. Using small 0.1s steps ensures the button remains responsive.
*   **Variable Decay**: Forgetting to decrement `timeLeft` in the loop will leave the light ON forever after the first press.
*   **Arithmetic Precision**: Calculations with decimals (0.1) in loops can sometimes accumulate tiny errors; check that your condition is `> 0` for reliability.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Warning Flash (Fade)**: Use PWM to make the LED dim gradually during the last 2 seconds to warn the user that the light is about to turn off.
*   **Extended Duration**: Change the set-point to 60 seconds to simulate a real-world hallway or basement stairs timer.
*   **Manual Off**: If the button is pressed *while* the light is already ON, make the timer reset to 0 to turn it off immediately.

## Project 0020: Mastering Button Logic (Software Debounce)

### 2. Learning Objective
Program a debounced button handler. Write software to filter mechanical switch bounce noise, ensuring each press registers cleanly as exactly one event.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Signal Noise** (Used in Step 8): Understanding mechanical switch bounce.
*   **Software Filtering** (Used in Step 8): Using code to ignore brief, invalid spikes.
*   **Reliability** (Used in Step 8): Ensuring consistent input handling for user interfaces.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Pushbutton**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Button** | GP14 | Pull-Down required for clean Low state |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`controls_if`** (if)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`change [variable] by 1`** (math_change)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_read`** (read Pin)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_log`** (log/print)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`** (wait)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`repeat while`** (controls_whileUntil)
*   From **Text**, drag **`""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **btn**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)` representing GP14 for input control.
*   **count**: Number variable used to track the clean, debounced click count.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variables**:
    *   Create variables **`btn`** and **`count`**.
    *   Set **`btn`** to **`Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
2.  **Initialize State**:
    *   Set **`count`** to **0**.
    *   **Snap** into the **`start`** block.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
3.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization section.
4.  **Detect Press Event**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** block.
    *   **Condition**: Check if **`pico_gpio_read`** for **`btn`** equals **1**.
5.  **Handle Event & Debounce**:
    *   Inside the "do" part of Step 4:
        1.  **Noise Settle**: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.05** seconds.
        2.  **Verify**: From **Logic & Math**, drag a nested **`controls_if`**. **Condition**: Check if **`pico_gpio_read`** for **`btn`** still equals **1**.
        3.  **Action**: 
            *   From **Variables**, drag **`change [count] by 1`**. 
            *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_log`**. Enter **"Click"** and the variable **`count`**.
        4.  **Wait for Release**: From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`repeat while`** block.
        5.  **Condition**: Check if **`pico_gpio_read`** for **`btn`** equals **1**.
        6.  **Action**: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.01** seconds.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Signal**: Pico sees HIGH.
2.  **Wait**: Pauses 50ms to let noise settle.
3.  **Verify**: Checks again. If still HIGH -> Valid Press.
4.  **Action**: Counts and Prints.
5.  **Release**: Waits for button to go LOW before accepting next press.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin
import time

# Initialization
btn = Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
count = 0

while True:
    if btn.value():
        # Step 1: Detect initial spike and wait (Noise Settle Period)
        time.sleep(0.05) 
        
        # Step 2: Re-verify signal state (Avoid false triggers)
        if btn.value():  
            count += 1
            print("Click", count)
            
            # Step 3: Wait for physical release (Edge Detection)
            while btn.value(): 
                time.sleep(0.01)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Settling Window Too Long**: If the noise-settle wait is greater than 0.1s, the button feels "mushy" or sluggish to the user.
*   **Missing Release Loop**: Without the `while` loop to wait for the release, a single long press will keep incrementing the counter at high speed.
*   **Idle Polling**: Failure to include a tiny wait in the release loop (like `0.01s`) can sometimes cause the Pico to draw more power than necessary while idling.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Hardware Filtering**: Add a 1uF capacitor across the button terminals. This physically smooths the electrical noise (RC filter).
*   **Dynamic Debounce**: Create a variable `debounceTime` and try to find the smallest possible value that still prevents double-counting.
*   **Event Logging**: Add a second LED that blinks once *only* when a valid, debounced click is confirmed.

## Project 0021: Introduction to Sound & Music

### 2. Learning Objective
Generate audio output. Control an Active Buzzer to make simple beep sounds, learning that "Sound" is just turning voltage on and off.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Active vs Passive Buzzers** (Used in Step 8): Distinguishing between self-oscillating (Active) components and frequency-dependent (Passive) ones.
*   **Auditory Feedback** (Used in Step 8): Providing system status information via sound rather than vision.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Active Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Buzzer (+)** | GP15 | Positive Terminal (Long Leg); requires HIGH to sound |
| **Buzzer (-)** | GND | Negative Terminal (Short Leg) |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin to ...)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`** (wait)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   From **Text**, drag **`""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **buzzer**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(15, Pin.OUT)` representing the Active Buzzer on GP15.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variable**:
    *   Create variable **`buzzer`**.
    *   Set **`buzzer`** to **`Pin(15, Pin.OUT)`**.
    *   **Snap** into the **`start`** block.
2.  **Initial State**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**.
    *   Set Pin to **`buzzer`** and State to **0**. (Ensuring silence at startup).

**B. Main Loop Phase**
3.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization section.
4.  **Audio Pulse (ON)**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **15** (buzzer) and State to **HIGH (1)**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.5** seconds.
5.  **Audio Pulse (OFF)**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **15** (buzzer) and State to **LOW (0)**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.5** seconds.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Logic**: Identical to blinking an LED, but instead of light, the component vibrates air. 
2.  **Signal**: High Voltage (GPIO 1) triggers the internal oscillator of the active buzzer.
3.  **Result**: 0.5s of sound followed by 0.5s of silence.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin
import time

# Initialization
buzzer = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)
buzzer.value(0) # Ensure OFF at start

while True:
    buzzer.value(1) # Beep ON (High Voltage triggers Active Buzzer)
    time.sleep(0.5)
    buzzer.value(0) # Beep OFF (Silence)
    time.sleep(0.5)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Active vs Passive Confusion**: If applying power only results in a faint "click" instead of a continuous tone, you likely have a **Passive Buzzer**. These require a rapid PWM signal to vibrate the disc. Active buzzers (used here) have a built-in oscillator.
*   **Polarity**: Buzzers are polarized. If you connect them backwards, they may not sound. The long leg is positive (+).
*   **Missing Silence Delay**: If you forget the second `wait` block, the buzzer will turn OFF and then immediately ON again, making it sound like it's never turning off.

> [!IMPORTANT]
> Always verify if your buzzer has a "remove seal" sticker. If it is still on, the sound will be significantly muffled.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Morse Code**: Beep S-O-S (... --- ...). Simple timing challenge by varying the wait times.
*   **Fast Pulse**: Reduce the wait times to 0.1s to create a "Rapid Alert" sound.
*   **Heartbeat Pattern**: Create a "Lub-Dub" rhythm by using uneven wait times (0.2s ON, 0.2s OFF, 0.2s ON, 0.6s OFF).

## Project 0022: Blinking Sound (Sync)

### 2. Learning Objective
Synchronize multiple outputs. Ensure an LED and a Buzzer fire at the exact same time, creating a cohesive "Alarm" effect.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Parallel Actuation** (Used in Step 8): Driving multiple pins in immediate succession.
*   **Perceived Parallelism** (Used in Step 8): Understanding that MicroPython executes code line-by-line, but at speeds faster than human perception.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **LED**
*   **Active Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Buzzer** | GP15 | Shared alarm indicator (Active Buzzer) |
| **LED** | GP14 | Visual alarm indicator |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin to ...)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`** (wait)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   From **Text**, drag **`""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **buzzer**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(15, Pin.OUT)` for audio output.
*   **led**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(14, Pin.OUT)` for visual output.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variables**:
    *   Create variables **`led`** and **`buzzer`**.
    *   Set **`led`** to **`Pin(14, Pin.OUT)`**.
    *   Set **`buzzer`** to **`Pin(15, Pin.OUT)`**.
    *   **Snap** into the **`start`** block.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
**B. Main Loop Phase**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization section.
3.  **Synchronized ON Phase**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **14** (led) and State to **HIGH (1)**.
    *   Below that, drag another **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **15** (buzzer) and State to **HIGH (1)**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.5** seconds.
4.  **Synchronized OFF Phase**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **14** (led) and State to **LOW (0)**.
    *   Below that, drag another **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **15** (buzzer) and State to **LOW (0)**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.5** seconds.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **CPU Speed**: The Pico executes instructions at MHz speeds (millions per second).
2.  **Sequential Delay**: Although "Turn LED On" happens nanoseconds BEFORE "Turn Buzzer On", the human eye/ear cannot distinguish this delay.
3.  **Perception**: Both outputs appear to trigger at the exact same moment, creating a unified alert experience.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin
import time

# Initialization
led = Pin(14, Pin.OUT)
buzzer = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)

while True:
    # Triggering both at the same time (no sleep between them)
    led.value(1)
    buzzer.value(1)
    time.sleep(0.5)
    
    led.value(0)
    buzzer.value(0)
    time.sleep(0.5)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Sequential vs Simultaneous**: If you put a `wait` between turning the LED on and the Buzzer on, they will drift out of sync. Group commands together for the best effect.
*   **Incorrect Pins**: GP14 and GP15 are adjacent on the Pico. Double-check your wiring to ensure the LED isn't on the Buzzer pin and vice versa.
*   **Inverted Off Logic**: Ensure both are set to **0** in the "Off Phase"; if one is accidentally left at **1**, it will stay on while the other blinks.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Alternating Alert**: Set the LED to ON and Buzzer to OFF, then swap them in the second phase (LED OFF, Buzzer ON).
*   **Double-Pulse LED**: Make the LED blink twice quickly during the "Buzzer ON" phase.
*   **Panic Mode**: Use an `if` block with a button to trigger this alarm only when pressed.

## Project 0023: Manual Sound Control (Doorbell)

### 2. Learning Objective
Create a user-interactive sound device. The buzzer sounds only while the button is pressed, behaving exactly like a basic doorbell.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Direct Mapping** (Used in Step 8): Explicitly linking an input state (Button) to an output state (Buzzer) without persistent memory or counters.
*   **Momentary Action** (Used in Step 8): Behavior that only exists as long as physical contact is maintained.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Pushbutton**
*   **Active Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Button** | GP14 | Doorbell Trigger (Input with Internal Pull-Down) |
| **Buzzer** | GP15 | Doorbell Sounder (Active Buzzer on Output) |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`controls_if`** (if / else branch)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin to ...)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_read`** (read Pin)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   From **Text**, drag **`""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **btn**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)` for input control.
*   **buzzer**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(15, Pin.OUT)` for audio output.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variables**:
    *   Create variables **`btn`** and **`buzzer`**.
    *   Set **`btn`** to **`Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
    *   Set **`buzzer`** to **`Pin(15, Pin.OUT)`**.
    *   **Snap** into the **`start`** block.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization section.
3.  **Doorbell Logic (Mirroring Input)**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** block. Click the gear to add an **else** branch.
    *   **If Branch (Pressed)**:
        *   **Condition**: Check if **`pico_gpio_read`** for **`btn`** equals **1**.
        *   **Action**: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **15** (buzzer) and State to **HIGH (1)**.
    *   **Else Branch (Released)**:
        *   **Action**: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **15** (buzzer) and State to **LOW (0)**.

### 9. Execution Flow
The buzzer mirrors the button. Button Down -> Buzz. Button Up -> Silence.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin
import time

# Initialization
btn = Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
buzzer = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)

while True:
    # Direct Output-to-Input mirroring
    if btn.value():
        buzzer.value(1) # Sound while contact is made
    else:
        buzzer.value(0) # Silence when contact is broken
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Buzzer Latching**: Students sometimes write code that turns the buzzer ON but forgets to turn it OFF when the button is released, leading to an endless screech.
*   **Internal Pull-Down Missing**: Without the pull-down resistor, the input pin will float, causing the doorbell to ring randomly even when the button isn't touched.
*   **Inverted Logic**: Make sure the sound happens in the "If" block ( HIGH) and silence happens in the "Else" block (LOW).

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Ding-Dong Sequence**: Instead of a simple ON/OFF, when the button is pressed, play a short sequence of two distinct pulses (0.2s ON, 0.1s OFF, 0.2s ON).
*   **Visual Doorbell**: Add an LED that lights up whenever the buzzer is sounding to help those with hearing difficulties.
*   **Panic Button**: Modify the code so that once the button is pressed, the buzzer keeps sounding for 5 seconds even after the button is released.

## Project 0024: Sound Sequences (Passive Melody)

### 2. Learning Objective
Generate specific frequencies. Use a **Passive Buzzer** and Pulse Width Modulation (PWM) to sing specific musical notes (Do, Re, Mi) in a sequence.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Frequency vs Duty Cycle** (Used in Step 8): Frequency determines pitch (Hz); Duty Cycle determines volume/presence.
*   **PWM Audio** (Used in Step 8): Using Pulse Width Modulation to drive a passive piezo element.
*   **Musical Notation** (Used in Step 8): Mapping specific frequencies to standard notes (e.g., C4 = 262Hz).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Passive Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Buzzer (+)** | GP15 | Positive terminal; requires PWM for pitch control (Passive) |
| **Buzzer (-)** | GND | Negative terminal; ground connection |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   From **Motion & Motors**, drag **`play note`** (pico_music)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`** (wait)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   From **Text**, drag **`""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **buzzer**: PWM object initialized on GP15 for precise frequency control.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variable**:
    *   Create variable **`buzzer`**.
    *   Set **`buzzer`** to **`PWM(Pin(15))`**.
    *   **Snap** into the **`start`** block.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization section.
3.  **Play Melody Phase**:
    *   From **Motion & Motors**, drag the **`play note`** block.
    *   **Settings**: Set Note to **C4**, Duration to **0.5** seconds, and Pin to **buzzer**.
    *   Repeat Step 3 for Note **D4** and Note **E4**.
4.  **Silence Gap**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **1.0** seconds.

### 9. Execution Flow
The Pico vibrates the speaker membrane 262 times a second, then 294 times, then 330. Our brain interprets these vibration speeds as musical notes.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin, PWM
import time

# Initialization
buzzer = PWM(Pin(15))

def play(freq, duration):
    """Utility to set frequency and 50% duty cycle for optimal clarity"""
    buzzer.freq(freq)
    buzzer.duty_u16(32768) # 50% duty cycle (Maximum volume for square wave)
    time.sleep(duration)
    buzzer.duty_u16(0)     # Duty 0 cuts power (Silence)

while True:
    play(262, 0.5) # C4
    play(294, 0.5) # D4
    play(330, 0.5) # E4
    time.sleep(1) # Gap between melody loops
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Active Buzzer Limitation**: If used here, it will just make a weird "squeak" or constant tone, ignoring the frequency changes. You MUST use a Passive variant for melodies.
*   **Duty Cycle Volume**: For sound, 50% duty is loudest. 100% duty is DC voltage which results in silence and potential heat damage to the piezo element.
*   **Blocking Code**: The `pico_music` block is "blocking," meaning the Pico waits for the note to finish before moving to the next line. You cannot trigger other actions *during* the note with this specific block.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Songwriter**: Code "Twinkle Twinkle Little Star" (CC GGA AG...) by copying and changing the notes in the sequence.
*   **Tempo Variable**: Create a variable `tempo` and use it as the duration for all notes to speed up or slow down the song easily.
*   **Rising Siren**: Use a `for` loop (from Loops) to play a range of frequencies (e.g., 200 to 1000 Hz) to create a sliding siren effect.

## Project 0025: Interactive Sound (Piano)

### 2. Learning Objective
Map inputs to pitches. Build a 2-key keyboard where Button A plays a low note and Button B plays a high note.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Input-to-Pitch Mapping** (Used in Step 8): Assigning distinct digital inputs to specific PWM frequencies.
*   **Priority Logic** (Used in Step 8): Using an `if / else if` chain to decide which sound wins if multiple keys are pressed.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Button A, Button B**
*   **Passive Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Button A** | GP10 | Piano Key 1 (Requires Pull-Down) |
| **Button B** | GP11 | Piano Key 2 (Requires Pull-Down) |
| **Buzzer** | GP15 | Audio Output (Passive Buzzer) |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`controls_if`** (if / else if / else)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_read`** (read Pin)
*   From **Motion & Motors**, drag **`play note`** (pico_music)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   From **Text**, drag **`""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **btnA**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(10, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)` for input.
*   **btnB**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(11, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)` for input.
*   **buzzer**: PWM object initialized on GP15 for pitch generation.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variables**:
    *   Create variables **`btnA`**, **`btnB`**, and **`buzzer`**.
    *   Set **`btnA`** to **`Pin(10, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
    *   Set **`btnB`** to **`Pin(11, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
    *   Set **`buzzer`** to **`PWM(Pin(15))`**.
    *   **Snap** into the **`start`** block.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization section.
3.  **Piano Key Logic**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** block. Click the gear to add `else if` and `else` branches.
    *   **Branch 1 (A)**: If **`pico_gpio_read`** for **`btnA`** equals **1**:
        *   From **Motion & Motors**, drag **`play note`**. Set Note to **C4**, Duration to **0.5s**, and Pin to **buzzer**.
    *   **Branch 2 (B)**: Else If **`pico_gpio_read`** for **`btnB`** equals **1**:
        *   From **Motion & Motors**, drag **`play note`**. Set Note to **G4**, Duration to **0.5s**, and Pin to **buzzer**.
    *   **Branch 3 (Idle)**: Else:
        *   From **Motion & Motors**, drag **`play note`**. Set Pin to **buzzer** and select the **Stop Sound** (Duty 0) setting.

### 9. Execution Flow
The buzzer waits. Pressing a key sets the vibration speed immediately. Releasing it kills the vibration. You can play rhythmic tunes by tapping the buttons.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
from machine import Pin, PWM
import time

# Initialization
buzzer = PWM(Pin(15))
btnA = Pin(10, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
btnB = Pin(11, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)

while True:
    # Priority check: Only one note can play at a time
    if btnA.value():
        buzzer.freq(262)
        buzzer.duty_u16(32768)
    elif btnB.value():
        buzzer.freq(392)
        buzzer.duty_u16(32768)
    else:
        # Duty 0 stops the physical vibration
        buzzer.duty_u16(0)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Laggy Response**: If the loop runs too slow (due to large sleeps elsewhere), you might hear "clicking" or experience delays between pressing and hearing sound.
*   **Polyphony Limitation**: The Pico (and this code) can only play one note at a time on a single buzzer. Priority logic (A before B) decides which note wins if both keys are hit.
*   **Silent Passive Buzzer**: Ensure you are using `pico_music` or PWM blocks. Using a simple `pico_gpio_write` (HIGH) on a passive buzzer will result in no sound.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Octave Shift**: Use a 3rd button to shift everything up an Octave by multiplying the frequencies.
*   **Sharp/Flat Key**: Add a 3rd button that plays C#4 (C-sharp) to learn about semitones.
*   **Visual Key**: Add two LEDs (Red and Blue). Red flashes when Button A is pressed; Blue flashes for Button B.

## Project 0026: Smart Sound Switch (Mute)

### 2. Learning Objective
Control system settings with a persistent flag. Make an alarm that runs continuously, but can be silenced (Muted) by toggling a switch.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Persistent Flags** (Used in Step 8): Using a variable to store a state (True/False) that survives across multiple loop cycles.
*   **Logic Inversion (`NOT`)** (Used in Step 8): Flipping a boolean state or ignoring a code block based on a condition.
*   **Software Muting** (Used in Step 8): Using software logic to disable hardware without physically cutting wires.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Button**
*   **Active Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Button** | GP14 | Mute Toggle Switch |
| **Buzzer** | GP15 | Alarm Output (Active Buzzer) |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`controls_if`** (if / else)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`not`** (logic_negate)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_read`** (read Pin)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin to ...)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`** (wait)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`false`** (logic_boolean)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`repeat while`** (controls_whileUntil)
*   From **Text**, drag **`""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **isMuted**: Boolean variable used to track if the alarm should be silent.
*   **btn**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)` for the toggle.
*   **buzzer**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(15, Pin.OUT)` for the alarm sound.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variables**:
    *   Create variables **`btn`**, **`buzzer`**, and **`isMuted`**.
    *   Set **`btn`** to **`Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
    *   Set **`buzzer`** to **`Pin(15, Pin.OUT)`**.
    *   Set **`isMuted`** to **false**.
    *   **Snap** into the **`start`** block.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization section.
3.  **Toggle Mute Feature**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** block.
    *   **Condition**: Check if **`pico_gpio_read** for **`btn`** equals **1**:
        *   **Toggle**: From **Variables**, drag **`set [isMuted] to`**. From **Logic & Math**, drag **`not`** and snap variable **`isMuted`** inside.
        *   **Wait for Release**: From **Logic & Math**, drag the **`repeat while`** block.
        *   **Condition**: Check if **`pico_gpio_read`** for **`btn`** equals **1**:
            *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.1** seconds.
4.  **Conditional Alarm Phase**:
    *   Below the toggle logic: From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** block with an **else** branch.
    *   **Condition**: Check if **NOT** **`isMuted`** (drag **`not`** from **Logic & Math** and snap **`isMuted`**).
    *   **Action (Beep Sequence)**:
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **15** (buzzer) and State to **HIGH (1)**.
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.1** seconds.
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **15** (buzzer) and State to **LOW (0)**.
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.9** seconds.
    *   **Else (Muted)**:
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **1.0** seconds. (Idle quiet period).

### 9. Execution Flow
The alarm logic runs constantly. However, before it plays a sound, it checks the "Mute" rule. If Mute is On, it skips the sound and just waits quietly. This demonstrates how software can override hardware behaviors.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin
import time

btn = Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
buzzer = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)
isMuted = False

while True:
    if btn.value():
        isMuted = not isMuted
        while btn.value(): time.sleep(0.1) # Wait release
        
    if not isMuted:
        buzzer.value(1)
        time.sleep(0.1)
        buzzer.value(0)
        time.sleep(0.9)
    else:
        time.sleep(1.0)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Responsiveness**: If the beep/wait cycle is too long (e.g., 2 seconds), pressing the Mute button might respond slowly because the code is stuck in `pico_wait`.
*   **Variable Scope**: Ensure `isMuted` is initialized outside the main loop. If you initialize it *inside* the loop, it will reset to `false` every time the loop restarts.
*   **Missing Release Loop**: If you forget to wait for the button release, the code will toggle the mute state dozens of times per second while you hold the button.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Visual Mute Indicator**: Light up a Red LED when Mute is active so the user knows the alarm is still "armed" but just silent.
*   **Mute Duration**: Change the code so that Mute only lasts for 10 seconds before automatically turning back on.
*   **Double-Click Mute**: Require two fast clicks of the button to toggle the mute state to prevent accidental silences.

## Project 0027: Sound Alarm System (Reverse Truck)

### 2. Learning Objective
Modulate frequency based on danger. A "Reverse Alarm" beeps slowly normally. When a sensor (simulated by button) triggers, it beeps frantically.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Context Awareness** (Used in Step 8): Changing behavior based on environmental triggers (Safe vs Danger).
*   **Variable Timing (DRY)** (Used in Step 8): Using a single block of code with a variable delay to handle multiple states without repetition.
*   **Dynamic Response** (Used in Step 8): Feedback that scales in intensity with the frequency of the input.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Button**
*   **Active Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Button** | GP14 | Danger Sensor Simulator |
| **Buzzer** | GP15 | Alarm Output (Active Buzzer) |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`controls_if`** (if / else)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin to ...)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_read`** (read Pin)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`** (wait)
*   From **Text**, drag **`""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **btn**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)` representing the proximity sensor.
*   **buzzer**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(15, Pin.OUT)` for the audio alert.
*   **delayTime**: Number variable used to control the beeping tempo.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variables**:
    *   Create variables **`btn`**, **`buzzer`**, and **`delayTime`**.
    *   Set **`btn`** to **`Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
    *   Set **`buzzer`** to **`Pin(15, Pin.OUT)`**.
    *   Set **`delayTime`** to **1.0**.
    *   **Snap** into the **`start`** block.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization section.
3.  **The "Safety Check" Logic**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** block with an **else** branch.
    *   **If Branch (Danger)**: Check if **`pico_gpio_read`** for **`btn`** equals **1**.
        *   From **Variables**, drag **`set [delayTime] to`** 0.1.
    *   **Else (Safe)**:
        *   From **Variables**, drag **`set [delayTime] to`** 1.0.
4.  **The Beep Phase (Reusable Logic)**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **15** (buzzer) and State to **HIGH (1)**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Snap variable **`delayTime`** into the duration field.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **15** (buzzer) and State to **LOW (0)**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Snap variable **`delayTime`** into the duration field.

### 9. Execution Flow
The code sets the "Pace" of the loop based on the button. Then it executes the Beep-Wait sequence using that pace. This efficiently handles both fast and slow modes with one block of code.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin
import time

# Initialization
btn = Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
buzzer = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)
delayTime = 1.0 # Default safe pace

while True:
    # Phase 1: Context Check
    if btn.value():
        delayTime = 0.1 # Urgent frantic pace for danger
    else:
        delayTime = 1.0 # Calm slow pace for safe operation
        
    # Phase 2: Single Execution Path (DRY)
    # The same code block handles both modes relative to delayTime
    buzzer.value(1) # Sound ON
    time.sleep(delayTime)
    buzzer.value(0) # Sound OFF
    time.sleep(delayTime)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Logic Separation**: Don't write two separate Beep logic blocks (one for fast, one for slow). Write one beep block and use a variable for the speed. It's cleaner and follows the DRY (Don't Repeat Yourself) principle.
*   **Variable Type**: Ensure `delayTime` is initialized as a number (e.g., `1.0`) and not a string (`"1.0"`), otherwise the `wait` block will fail.
*   **Sensor Polarity**: If your "Danger" button is Pulse-Up (HIGH when pressed), use a Pull-Down. If it's Pulse-Down (LOW when pressed), invert the code logic.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Incremental Ramp**: Instead of two fixed speeds, make `delayTime` decrease by 0.1 every time the loop runs while the button is held, until it hits a minimum speed.
*   **Three-Stage Alarm**: Add a second button to simulate "Critical Danger" where the buzzer stays ON continuously.
*   **Visual Tempo**: Add an LED that blinks at the exact same tempo as the buzzer for visual reinforcement.

## Project 0028: The Sound Game (Simon Says)

### 2. Learning Objective
Memorize and repeat auditory patterns. The Pico plays a sequence (Low-Med-High). You must match it.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Sequence Memory** (Used in Step 8): Using a digital container (List) to store and recall an ordered set of values.
*   **Input Verification** (Used in Step 8): Comparing real-time human actions against a stored standard to decide success or failure.
*   **Auditory Patterns** (Used in Step 8): Using frequency variations to represent unique game events (Colors/Notes).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **3 Buttons**
*   **Passive Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Btn Low** | GP10 | Input for C4 note |
| **Btn Med** | GP11 | Input for E4 note |
| **Btn High** | GP12 | Input for G4 note |
| **Buzzer** | GP15 | Game Sounder (Passive) |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`repeat [3] times`** (controls_repeat_ext)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`create list with`** (lists_create_with)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`in list [sequence] get # [loop_index]`** (lists_getIndex)
*   From **Motion & Motors**, drag **`play note`** (pico_music)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`** (wait)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   From **Text**, drag **`""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **sequence**: List variable containing the notes [262, 330, 392] to be played.
*   **btnL**, **btnM**, **btnH**: Pin objects initialized for the three game keys.
*   **buzzer**: PWM object initialized on GP15 for melody playback.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variables**:
    *   Create variables **`btnL`**, **`btnM`**, **`btnH`**, and **`buzzer`**.
    *   Set **`btnL`** to **`Pin(10, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
    *   Set **`btnM`** to **`Pin(11, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
    *   Set **`btnH`** to **`Pin(12, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
    *   Set **`buzzer`** to **`PWM(Pin(15))`**.
2.  **Initialize Sequence**:
    *   Create variable **`sequence`**.
    *   Set to **List** with items: **262**, **330**, **392**.
    *   **Snap** all into the **`start`** block.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
3.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization section.
4.  **Computer Turn Phase**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`repeat [3] times`**.
        *   Inside loop: From **Logic & Math**, drag **`in list [sequence] get #`**. Use the loop variable for the index.
        *   From **Motion & Motors**, drag **`play note`**. Use the retrieved **Note** value, set Duration to **0.5s**, and Pin to **buzzer**.
        *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.1** seconds (Gap between notes).
5.  **Player Turn Phase**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **2.0** seconds.
    *   *(Note: This is the time window for the player to mimic the sound).*

### 9. Execution Flow
The Pico "Says" the pattern (plays tones). Then it listens. If you press the buttons in the matching pitch order, it plays a victory jingle.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin, PWM
import time

# Initialization
buzzer = PWM(Pin(15))
btnL = Pin(10, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
btnM = Pin(11, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
btnH = Pin(12, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)

# Frequencies: C4 (262), E4 (330), G4 (392)
sequence = [262, 330, 392]

def play_note(freq, duration):
    """Utility to map index values to audible tones"""
    buzzer.freq(freq)
    buzzer.duty_u16(32768)
    time.sleep(duration)
    buzzer.duty_u16(0)

while True:
    # Phase A: The computer 'speaks' the sequence
    print("Computer Turn...")
    for tone in sequence:
        play_note(tone, 0.5)
        time.sleep(0.1) # Short gap for clarity
        
    # Phase B: The user must repeat (Simplfied validation for this stage)
    print("Your Turn! (Listening for 2 seconds...)")
    time.sleep(2.0)
    
    # Note: Full input tracking is handled in Project 0035+ using Finite State Machines.
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Sequence Indexing**: Lists in many block platforms can be tricky. Ensure your loop index (1 to 3 or 0 to 2) correctly matches the items in your sequence list.
*   **Missing Tone Gap**: If you don't add a short `wait` (0.1s) after each note in the sequence, the tones will run together and sound like one long, confusing beep.
*   **Passive Buzzer Requirement**: An active buzzer cannot play the different frequencies required for this game. If you only hear one flat beep, check your buzzer type.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Randomization**: Use a **Math** block to pick a random frequency from a list of notes to make the game dynamic and unpredictable.
*   **Level Up**: Increase the sequence length by 1 every time the user successfully mimics the pattern.
*   **Victory Jingle**: Add a special `for` loop that plays a fast upward scale when the player wins.

## Project 0029: Automated Sound (Theremin)

### 2. Learning Objective
Convert analog data to frequency. Use a Light Sensor to alter pitch continuously. Waving your hand creates spooky "Theremin" music.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Mapping**: Converting Range A (0-65535 Light) to Range B (100-2000 Hz).
*   **Real-time response**: Instant auditory feedback.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **LDR Module**
*   **Passive Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LDR** | GP26 | Analog Light Sensor |
| **Buzzer** | GP15 | Audio Output (Passive Buzzer) |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag **`read analog sensor`** (pico_sensor_read)
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag **`map [X] from [A-B] to [C-D]`** (pico_map)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_pwm`** (pulse Pin ...)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`** (wait)
*   From **Text**, drag **`""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **val**: Number variable to store raw 16-bit analog data (0-65535).
*   **pitch**: Number variable to store the calculated audible frequency.
*   **buzzer**: PWM object initialized on GP15 for pitch output.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variables**:
    *   Create variables **`val`**, **`pitch`**, and **`buzzer`**.
    *   Set **`buzzer`** to **`PWM(Pin(15))`**.
    *   **Snap** into the **`start`** block.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization section.
3.  **Read & Map Light to Pitch**:
    *   From **Variables**, drag **`set [val] to`**. From **Smart Sensors**, drag **`read analog sensor`** and set to **GP26**.
    *   From **Variables**, drag **`set [pitch] to`**. From **Smart Sensors**, drag **`map [X] from [A-B] to [C-D]`**:
        *   **X**: variable **`val`**.
        *   **Input Range**: From **0** to **65535**.
        *   **Output Range**: From **100** to **2000**.
4.  **Play Continuous Tone**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_pwm`**. Set Pin to **15** (buzzer):
        *   **Duty**: 50% (**32768**).
        *   **Freq**: variable **`pitch`**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.05** seconds (Refresh rate).

### 9. Execution Flow
The darker it is, the lower the tone. The brighter, the higher. Because the loop waits only 0.05s, the pitch slides smoothly as you move your hand over the sensor.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin, PWM, ADC
import time

ldr = ADC(26)
buzzer = PWM(Pin(15))

def map_range(x, in_min, in_max, out_min, out_max):
    return (x - in_min) * (out_max - out_min) // (in_max - in_min) + out_min

while True:
    val = ldr.read_u16()
    pitch = map_range(val, 0, 65535, 100, 2000)
    
    buzzer.freq(pitch)
    buzzer.duty_u16(32768)
    time.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Zero Frequency Error**: If `pico_map` logic somehow produces a 0 or negative number, `pico_pwm` will crash. Always ensure your mapping minimum is at least 10-50Hz.
*   **Limited Room Range**: LDRs rarely reach full 0 or full 65535 in a normal room. If the pitch range feels too small, calibrate the input range (e.g., 20000 to 50000).
*   **Duty Cycle Volume**: For maximum Theremin effect, use a 50% duty cycle. Lowering this will make the sound "thinner" or softer.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Discrete Scale**: Instead of a smooth slide, use **Logic** to "snap" the pitch to the nearest entry in a list of musical notes (Auto-tune Theremin).
*   **Volume Control**: Add a second LDR on a different Pin and map it to the **Duty Cycle** to control volume with your other hand.
*   **Directional Sound**: Swap the LDR for an Ultrasonic sensor (HC-SR04) to control pitch based on distance.

## Project 0030: Mastering Sound (Mario Coin)

### 2. Learning Objective
Recreate a specific sound effect using precise envelopes. A "Coin" sound is B5 (988Hz) for 0.1s followed by E6 (1319Hz) for 0.3s.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Envelope**: How a sound changes over time (Attack, Decay, Sustain, Release) - simplified here to Pitch change.
*   **Timbre**: (Limited control, but pulse width affects it).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Passive Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Buzzer** | GP15 | SFX Output (Passive Buzzer) |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   From **Motion & Motors**, drag **`play note`** (pico_music)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`** (wait)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   From **Text**, drag **`""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **buzzer**: PWM object initialized on GP15 for sound effect generation.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variable**:
    *   Create variable **`buzzer`**.
    *   Set **`buzzer`** to **`PWM(Pin(15))`**.
    *   **Snap** into the **`start`** block.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization section.
3.  **Coin Sound Effect**:
    *   From **Motion & Motors**, drag the **`play note`** block. Set Note to **B5**, Duration to **0.1** seconds, and Pin to **buzzer**.
    *   Below that, drag another **`play note`** block. Set Note to **E6**, Duration to **0.3** seconds, and Pin to **buzzer**.
4.  **Gap Between Sounds**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **2.0** seconds.

### 9. Execution Flow
The rapid jump from high frequency to higher frequency creates the iconic "Bling!" sound. The timings must be short; too long and it sounds like a siren, not a coin.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin, PWM
import time

buzzer = PWM(Pin(15))

while True:
    # Note 1: B5 (988 Hz)
    buzzer.freq(988)
    buzzer.duty_u16(32768)
    time.sleep(0.1)
    
    # Note 2: E6 (1319 Hz)
    buzzer.freq(1319)
    buzzer.duty_u16(32768)
    time.sleep(0.3)
    
    # Silence
    buzzer.duty_u16(0)
    time.sleep(2.0)
```
### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Note Timing**: If the transition between B5 and E6 is too slow (e.g., 0.5s), it will lose the "Collect" feel and sound like a slow alarm. Keep the first note extremely short (0.1s or less).
*   **Passive Buzzer**: Using an active buzzer will prevent you from reaching the specific frequencies of B5 and E6, resulting in a generic buzz.
*   **Missing Silence**: If you forget the final `wait 2.0s` inside the loop, the coin sound will play repeatedly at high speed, becoming very annoying.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Power-Up Sound**: Create a "rising" sound by playing a sequence of 4 notes, each 50Hz higher than the previous one, in very quick succession (0.05s per note).
*   **Game Over Sound**: Play a long, low tone (200Hz) that gradually fades out using a loop that reduces the Duty Cycle.
*   **Button Trigger**: Wrap the sound effect in an `if` block so it only plays when a button (GP14) is pressed.

## Project 0031: Introduction to Simple Motors

### 2. Learning Objective
Learn to drive a high-current load. You will connect a DC Motor to the Pico using a driver (transistor/module), understanding why a microcontroller cannot drive a motor directly (current limits).

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Current Handling**: GPIOs are weak (20mA). Motors are hungry (200mA+).
*   **Driver/Transistor**: An electronic switch.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **DC Motor (with Fan blade)**
*   **Motor Driver Module (L9110 or similar)**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Driver IN-A** | GP14 | Control signal input (HIGH = Run) |
| **Driver VCC** | VBUS (5V) | Power for the motor driver |
| **Pico GND** | GND | Common ground reference |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin to ...)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`** (wait)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   From **Text**, drag **`""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **motor**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(14, Pin.OUT)` representing the motor driver control pin.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variable**:
    *   Create variable **`motor`**.
    *   Set **`motor`** to **`Pin(14, Pin.OUT)`**.
    *   **Snap** into the **`start`** block.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization section.
3.  **Spin Phase**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **14** (motor) and State to **HIGH (1)**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **3.0** seconds.
4.  **Stop Phase**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **14** (motor) and State to **LOW (0)**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **3.0** seconds.

### 9. Execution Flow
The Pico signals the Driver: "Turn on!". The Driver opens the floodgates from the battery to the motor. The fan spins. The Pico says "Stop!", and the Driver cuts the power.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin
import time

# Initialization
motor = Pin(14, Pin.OUT)

while True:
    motor.value(1) # Spin
    time.sleep(3)
    motor.value(0) # Stop
    time.sleep(3)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Direct Connection**: NEVER connect a motor directly to a Pico pin. It will burn out the pin or the whole chip. Always use a driver module or transistor to handle the high current.
*   **Shared Ground**: Failing to connect the ground (GND) of the motor driver to the Pico's GND. Without a shared reference, the signal won't trigger the driver.
*   **Voltage Drops**: If the Pico restarts when the motor spins up, the motor is drawing too much current from the USB bus. Use an external battery for the driver's VCC.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Variable Pulse**: Change the stop time to 1 second so the motor spins for longer than it rests.
*   **Morse Code Fan**: Create a sequence of short and long "Spin" cycles to communicate a letter through wind pulses.
*   **User Guard**: Add an 'if' check so the motor only cycles if a safety switch is held down.

## Project 0032: Blinking Simple Motors (Vibration)

### 2. Learning Objective
Pulse a motor effectively. You will turn a motor on and off rapidly to create haptic feedback (vibration), similar to a phone notification.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Haptics**: Communicating via touch.
*   **Inertia**: Motors don't stop instantly.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **DC Motor / Vibration Motor**
*   **Motor Driver**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Driver IN-A** | GP14 | Trigger signal for vibration |
| **Driver VCC** | VBUS/3V3 | Power supply for the vibration motor |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin to ...)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`** (wait)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   From **Text**, drag **`""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **motor**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(14, Pin.OUT)` for pulsing the vibration motor.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variable**:
    *   Create variable **`motor`**.
    *   Set **`motor`** to **`Pin(14, Pin.OUT)`**.
    *   **Snap** into the **`start`** block.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization section.
3.  **Vibration Pulse**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **14** (motor) and State to **HIGH (1)**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.5** seconds.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **14** (motor) and State to **LOW (0)**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.5** seconds.

### 9. Execution Flow
The motor spins up briefly, shaking the device, then spins down. Repeating this creates a "Bzzt... Bzzt..." feeling.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin
import time

# Initialization
motor = Pin(14, Pin.OUT)

while True:
    motor.value(1) # Start vibration
    time.sleep(0.5)
    motor.value(0) # Stop vibration
    time.sleep(0.5)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Flyback Diode**: If building raw circuits, remember a diode across the motor to protect the Pico from high voltage spikes when the motor stops. Motor drivers usually have these built-in.
*   **Duty Cycle Too Short**: If you pulse the motor too fast (e.g., 0.01s), the motor's inertia might prevent it from even starting to spin, resulting in silence or a faint hum.
*   **Weak Vibration**: Ensure the motor is physically mounted to something (like the breadboard or a case) to transfer the vibration energy, otherwise it will just spin quietly in the air.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **SOS Haptics**: Create a sequence of 3 short pulses, 3 long pulses, and 3 short pulses.
*   **Intensity Ramp**: Change the wait times to get progressively shorter, making the vibration feel more "urgent."
*   **Double-Tap**: Create a "heartbeat" pulse pattern with two very quick bursts followed by a longer silence.

## Project 0033: Manual Motor Control (Fan)

### 2. Learning Objective
User-controlled actuation. The fan runs only when the button is held.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Deadman Switch**: Action only on hold.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Pushbutton**
*   **DC Motor + Driver**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Button** | GP10 | Fan Trigger (Requires Pull-Down) |
| **Driver IN-A** | GP14 | Motor Driver Control Signal |
| **Ground** | GND | Common Ground reference |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`controls_if`** (if / else)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_read`** (read Pin)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin to ...)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`** (wait)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   From **Text**, drag **`""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **btn**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(10, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)` for user input.
*   **motor**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(14, Pin.OUT)` to control the fan.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variables**:
    *   Create variables **`btn`** and **`motor`**.
    *   Set **`btn`** to **`Pin(10, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
    *   Set **`motor`** to **`Pin(14, Pin.OUT)`**.
    *   **Snap** into the **`start`** block.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization section.
3.  **Fan Control Logic**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** block. Click the gear to add an **else** branch.
    *   **If Branch (Pressed)**:
        *   **Condition**: Check if **`pico_gpio_read`** for **`btn`** equals **1**.
        *   **Action**: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **14** (motor) and State to **HIGH (1)**.
    *   **Else Branch (Released)**:
        *   **Action**: From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_write`**. Set Pin to **14** (motor) and State to **LOW (0)**.
4.  **CPU Relief**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.01** seconds. (Ensuring loop stability).

### 9. Execution Flow
Press button -> Wind blows. Release button -> Wind stops.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin
import time

# Initialization
btn = Pin(10, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
motor = Pin(14, Pin.OUT)

while True:
    if btn.value():
        motor.value(1) # Run fan while button is held
    else:
        motor.value(0) # Stop fan on release
    time.sleep(0.01)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Power Hunger**: If the fan slows down or the Pico resets when the button is held, your USB power supply is likely insufficient. Use a powered hub or external power for the driver.
*   **Missing Else**: If you forget the `else` block, the motor will turn ON but never turn OFF, even after you release the button.
*   **Pin Floating**: If you forget the `PULL_DOWN` settings, the fan might spin randomly due to static electricity on the wire.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Toggle Mode**: Modify the code so that one click turns the fan ON permanently, and the next click turns it OFF.
*   **Safety Timer**: Add a variable so that the fan runs for a maximum of 5 seconds per press to prevent overheating.
*   **Speed Indicator**: Add an LED that turns on whenever the motor is active to provide visual status.

## Project 0034: Simple Motors Sequences (Variable Speed)

### 2. Learning Objective
Control motor speed using Software. You will cycle through Low, Medium, and High speeds using PWM.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **PWM (Pulse Width Modulation)**: Simulating lower voltage by pulsing fast.
*   **Duty Cycle**: % of time the power is ON.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Button**
*   **DC Motor + Driver (PWM support needed)**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Button** | GP10 | Speed Cycle Trigger (Pull-Down) |
| **Driver IN-A** | GP14 | PWM Control Input for Speed |
| **Driver VCC** | VBUS | 5V Power for Motor |
| **Ground** | GND | Common Ground reference |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`controls_if`** (if / else if / else)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set [variable] to`** (variable assignment)
*   From **Variables**, drag **`change [variable] by [1]`** (math_change)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_gpio_read`** (read Pin)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_pwm`** (pulse Pin ...)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`repeat while`** (controls_whileUntil)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`** (wait)
*   From **Logic & Math**, drag **`[X] > [Y]`** (logic_compare)
*   From **Text**, drag **`""`** (text string for direct Pin definition)

### 7. Variables
*   **speedLevel**: Number variable (0-3) used to track the current speed state.
*   **btn**: Pin object initialized as `Pin(10, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)` for speed switching.
*   **motor**: PWM object initialized on GP14 for variable speed power delivery.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Hardware Variables**:
    *   Create variables **`btn`**, **`motor`**, and **`speedLevel`**.
    *   Set **`btn`** to **`Pin(10, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)`**.
    *   Set **`motor`** to **`PWM(Pin(14))`**.
    *   Set **`speedLevel`** to **0**.
    *   **Snap** into the **`start`** block.
2.  **Configure Motor Frequency**:
    *   Set **`motor`** frequency to **100Hz** (Ideal for DC motors).

**B. Main Loop Phase**
3.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag the **`pico_forever`** block.
    *   **Snap** below the initialization section.
4.  **Cycle Speed on Click**:
    *   From **Logic & Math**, drag a **`controls_if`** block.
    *   **Condition**: Check if **`pico_gpio_read`** for **`btn`** equals **1**:
        *   From **Variables**, drag **`change [speedLevel] by 1`**.
        *   **Reset Boundary**: From **Logic & Math**, drag a nested **`controls_if`**. Check if **`speedLevel`** > **3**. If true, set **`speedLevel`** to **0**.
        *   **Debounce/Wait Release**: From **Logic & Math**, drag **`repeat while`**.
        *   **Condition**: Check if **`pico_gpio_read`** for **`btn`** equals **1**:
            *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.01** seconds.
5.  **Apply Speed Logic**:
    *   Below the speed cycling logic: From **Logic & Math**, drag a multi-branch **`controls_if`**. Use the gear icon to add `else if` and `else` branches.
    *   **Level 0 (OFF)**: If **`speedLevel`** equals **0**, set **`motor`** duty to **0**.
    *   **Level 1 (LOW)**: Else if **`speedLevel`** equals **1**, set **`motor`** duty to **20000**.
    *   **Level 2 (MED)**: Else if **`speedLevel`** equals **2**, set **`motor`** duty to **40000**.
    *   **Level 3 (HIGH)**: Else, set **`motor`** duty to **65535**.
6.  **Loop Stability**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag **`pico_wait`**. Set duration to **0.1** seconds.

### 9. Execution Flow
The user clicks to cycle speeds. The Pico sends a signal that is "mostly off" for Low speed, "half on" for Medium, and "fully on" for High. The motor averages this out and spins at different rates.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import Pin, PWM
import time

# Initialization
btn = Pin(10, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
motor = PWM(Pin(14))
motor.freq(100) # 100Hz is suitable for most DC motors

speedLevel = 0

while True:
    if btn.value():
        speedLevel += 1
        if speedLevel > 3:
            speedLevel = 0
            
        # Debounce: Wait for button release
        while btn.value():
            time.sleep(0.01)
            
    # Apply PWM Speed according to Level
    if speedLevel == 0: 
        motor.duty_u16(0)
    elif speedLevel == 1: 
        motor.duty_u16(20000)
    elif speedLevel == 2: 
        motor.duty_u16(40000)
    else: 
        motor.duty_u16(65535)
    
    time.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Stall Window**: DC motors have an inertia threshold. At very low duty cycles (like 5000), the motor might hum but not physicaly turn. Always find your motor's minimum "spinning" duty cycle.
*   **Frequency Issues**: If the frequency is too low, the motor will "chug" or vibrate. If it's too high, the motor driver might overheat. 100Hz-500Hz is usually the sweet spot.
*   **Logic Drift**: If you forget the button release `while` loop, the speed will cycle uncontrollably through all four levels in a single brief press.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Smooth Ramp**: Instead of fixed steps, change the code so that holding the button slowly increases the duty cycle from 0 to 65535.
*   **Reverse Pin**: Use a second button to toggle the IA/IB states so you can have variable speed in both directions.
*   **Speed Indicator**: Add three LEDs that light up one-by-one to represent Low, Med, and High speed levels.
